The numerical information for the benchmark scenario of case
II
is provided in Table
6c
.
In this case, apart from the three light mostly-active neutrinos, there is also a mostly-sterile neutrino with a mass around
1
eV and a pair of quasi-degenerate neutrinos with masses close to
1
TeV.
The light neutrino mass spectrum in this scenario is similar to that of the conventional
\nu
MSM
[
119
,
120
]
. However, in the
\nu
MSM, the lightest neutrino has a mass of about 1 keV, making it a warm dark matter candidate, while the two heavier neutrinos with masses around 1 GeV generate the baryon asymmetry in the universe. In contrast, in the scenario under consideration, the lightest neutrino has a mass around 1 eV and the two heavier neutrinos have masses around 1 TeV.

From Table
6c
one sees that the symmetry in the last two columns of
KX^{\dagger}
is tied to an analogous symmetry in the last two rows of
X
and of
O_{c}
.
The latter can be understood from Eqs. (
2.21
) and (
2.18
).

The symmetry observed in the last two columns of
KX^{\dagger}
is also reflected in the last two rows of
X
and
O_{c}
, as seen in Table
6c
. This is due to the relationship between
X
,
O_{c}
, and
KX^{\dagger}
as described in Eqs. (
2.21
) and (
2.18
).

For the case
II
spectrum, one should consider
n=1
in the expression in Eq.(
2.45
). In a long-baseline experiment, the transition probability of muon to electron (anti-)neutrinos can be approximately expressed as described in Eq.(
2.45
)

\displaystyle P^{\text{LBL}}_{\hbox{\hskip 12.41393pt\hskip-2.00464pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.00464pt\hskip-12.41393pt\raisebox{4.06389pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-12.41393pt\hskip 12.41393pt}_{\mu}\rightarrow\hbox{\hskip 12.41393pt\hskip-2.00464pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.00464pt\hskip-12.41393pt\raisebox{4.06389pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-12.41393pt\hskip 12.41393pt}_{e}}\,\simeq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\Theta\Theta^{\dagger})_{\mu\mu}(\Theta\Theta^{\dagger})_{ee}}\Bigg[\left|(\Theta\Theta^{\dagger})_{\mu e}\right|^{2}-4\,\cdot\,\frac{1}{2}\,\re\left(\Theta_{\mu 4}^{*}\,\Theta_{e4}\,\sum_{j=1}^{3}\,\Theta_{\mu j}\,\Theta_{ej}^{*}\right)

(2.73)

\displaystyle-4\sum_{i>j}^{3}\,\re\left(\Theta_{\mu i}^{*}\,\Theta_{ei}\,\Theta_{\mu j}\,\Theta_{ej}^{*}\right)\sin^{2}\Delta_{ij}\pm 2\sum_{i>j}^{3}\,\im\left(\Theta_{\mu i}^{*}\,\Theta_{ei}\,\Theta_{\mu j}\,\Theta_{ej}^{*}\right)\sin 2\Delta_{ij}\Bigg]\,,

where terms depending on
\Delta_{4j}\gg 1

have been replaced by their averaged versions.
If the conditions are similar to those of the benchmark in Table
6c
where
|(\Theta\Theta^{\dagger}){\mu\mu}(\Theta\Theta^{\dagger}){ee}-1|
and
|(\Theta\Theta^{\dagger})_{\mu e}|^{2}
are negligible, the expression for the muon to electron (anti-)neutrino transition probability in a LBL context can be simplified to:

\displaystyle P^{\text{LBL}}_{\hbox{\hskip 12.41393pt\hskip-2.00464pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.00464pt\hskip-12.41393pt\raisebox{4.06389pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-12.41393pt\hskip 12.41393pt}_{\mu}\rightarrow\hbox{\hskip 12.41393pt\hskip-2.00464pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.00464pt\hskip-12.41393pt\raisebox{4.06389pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-12.41393pt\hskip 12.41393pt}_{e}}\,\simeq\,P^{\text{LBL, }3\nu}_{\hbox{\hskip 12.41393pt\hskip-2.00464pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.00464pt\hskip-12.41393pt\raisebox{4.06389pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-12.41393pt\hskip 12.41393pt}_{\mu}\rightarrow\hbox{\hskip 12.41393pt\hskip-2.00464pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.00464pt\hskip-12.41393pt\raisebox{4.06389pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-12.41393pt\hskip 12.41393pt}_{e}}+\frac{1}{2}\sin^{2}2\vartheta^{(4)}_{\mu e}+4\,\re\left(\Theta_{\mu 4}^{*}\,\Theta_{e4}\,(KX^{\dagger})_{\mu 2}\,(KX^{\dagger})_{e2}^{*}\right)\,.

(2.74)

In this case, the unitarity of the full
6\times 6
mixing matrix has been utilized, as well as the approximate symmetry
(KX^{\dagger}){\alpha 2}\simeq i,(KX^{\dagger}){\alpha 3}
. Additionally, if
|\Theta_{\alpha 4}|^{2}=|(KX^{\dagger}){\alpha 1}|^{2}\gg|(KX^{\dagger}){\alpha 2}|^{2}\simeq|(KX^{\dagger})_{\alpha 3}|^{2}
, the last term in the expression can be ignored, resulting in Eq. (
2.60
) from case
Ia
.

In a SBL context,
the relevant form of Eq. (
2.45
) for

\hbox{\hskip 15.50002pt\hskip-2.46991pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.46991pt\hskip-15.50002pt\raisebox{6.10555pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-15.50002pt\hskip 15.50002pt}_{\mu}\rightarrow\hbox{\hskip 15.50002pt\hskip-2.46991pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.46991pt\hskip-15.50002pt\raisebox{6.10555pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-15.50002pt\hskip 15.50002pt}_{e}

transitions in case
II
is:

\displaystyle P^{\text{SBL}}_{\hbox{\hskip 12.41393pt\hskip-2.00464pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.00464pt\hskip-12.41393pt\raisebox{4.06389pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-12.41393pt\hskip 12.41393pt}_{\mu}\rightarrow\hbox{\hskip 12.41393pt\hskip-2.00464pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.00464pt\hskip-12.41393pt\raisebox{4.06389pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-12.41393pt\hskip 12.41393pt}_{e}}\,\simeq\,\frac{1}{(\Theta\Theta^{\dagger})_{\mu\mu}(\Theta\Theta^{\dagger})_{ee}}\Bigg[\left|(\Theta\Theta^{\dagger})_{\mu e}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle-4\,\re\left(\Theta_{\mu 4}^{*}\,\Theta_{e4}\,\sum_{j=1}^{3}\,\Theta_{\mu j}\,\Theta_{ej}^{*}\right)\sin^{2}\Delta_{41}

(2.75)

\displaystyle\pm 2\,\im\left(\Theta_{\mu 4}^{*}\,\Theta_{e4}\,\sum_{j=1}^{3}\,\Theta_{\mu j}\,\Theta_{ej}^{*}\right)\sin 2\Delta_{41}\Bigg]\,,

with
\Delta_{41}\simeq\Delta_{42}\simeq\Delta_{43}
.

One is sensitive to oscillations due to the mass-squared differences
\Delta m^{2}_{4j}
with
j=1,2,3
, while the oscillations related to smaller mass-squared differences have not yet emerged.
In a situation resembling the numerical benchmark, this expression can be estimated as:

\displaystyle P^{\text{SBL}}_{\hbox{\hskip 12.41393pt\hskip-2.00464pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.00464pt\hskip-12.41393pt\raisebox{4.06389pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-12.41393pt\hskip 12.41393pt}_{\mu}\rightarrow\hbox{\hskip 12.41393pt\hskip-2.00464pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.00464pt\hskip-12.41393pt\raisebox{4.06389pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-12.41393pt\hskip 12.41393pt}_{e}}

\displaystyle\simeq\,\left[\sin^{2}2\vartheta^{(4)}_{\mu e}+8\,\re\left(\Theta_{\mu 4}^{*}\,\Theta_{e4}\,(KX^{\dagger})_{\mu 2}\,(KX^{\dagger})_{e2}^{*}\right)\right]\sin^{2}\Delta_{41}

(2.76)

\displaystyle\mp 4\,\im\left(\Theta_{\mu 4}^{*}\,\Theta_{e4}\,(KX^{\dagger})_{\mu 2}\,(KX^{\dagger})_{e2}^{*}\right)\sin 2\Delta_{41}\,,

where once again the unitarity of the full mixing matrix has been taken into account,
as well as the relation
(KX^{\dagger})_{\alpha 2}\simeq i\,(KX^{\dagger})_{\alpha 3}
.
If also
|(KX^{\dagger})_{\alpha 1}|^{2}\gg|(KX^{\dagger})_{\alpha 2}|^{2}\simeq|(KX^{\dagger})_{\alpha 3}|^{2}
,
then the two terms containing
(KX^{\dagger})_{\alpha 2}
in this equation
can be neglected and one recovers Eq. (
2.63
) of case
Ia
.

Figure 2.4
:

Active-sterile mixing measure
\sin^{2}2\vartheta_{\mu e}^{(4)}

versus
the lightest neutrino mass
m_{\text{min}}
from a scan of the case-
II
parameter space,
with NO (
m_{\text{min}}=m_{1}
). The heavy spectrum at tree level has

M_{1}=1
eV, while three values of the heaviest quasi-degenerate masses are considered,

M_{2}\simeq M_{3}=8\,v\,(14\,v)\,[20\,v]
,
corresponding to the black (dark blue) [light blue] points in the scatter plot.
Here,
v\simeq 174
GeV is the Higgs VEV.
The horizontal green band shows the
99.7\%
CL interval of Ref.
[
105
]
,
and the vertical red band corresponds to the cosmological constraint, as in Figure
2.2
.
The dark green contour delimits the region inside which loop-stable points have been found,
while the benchmark of Table
6c
is marked in yellow.

To further explore the parameter space of case
II
, numerical seesaw structures were generated resembling the benchmark in Table
6c
, by adopting a method similar to case
Ia
. It was set
M_{1}=1
eV at tree level and three different values for the second heaviest neutrino mass,
M_{2},(\simeq M_{3})\in{8,14,20}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ v
, where
v\simeq 174
GeV represents the Higgs VEV. The mass splitting
M_{3}-M_{2}
was varied within the range
[0.02,200]
eV. Figure
2.4
displays the values of
\sin^{2}2\vartheta_{\mu e}^{(4)}
in Eq. (
2.52
) plotted against the lightest neutrino mass for the numerical examples in case
II
. Only points for which
\textrm{Tr}\left[m\,m^{\dagger}\right]\in[0.001,\,1]\,v^{2}
are kept.
Similar to before, the green band across the horizontal axis represents the range of
\sin^{2}2\vartheta_{\mu e}^{(4)}
preferred by the global fit described in Ref.
[
105
]
. The dark green contour outlines the area where relatively loop-stable points are present.
The approximations used in deriving the oscillation formulae of Eqs. (
2.74
) and (
2.76
)
are valid for all the plotted points.

For the numerical examples of case
II
that remain stable under loop corrections, the values of
BR(\mu\rightarrow e\gamma)
can approach the upper bound of
4.2\times 10^{-13}
set by MEG. Our scan eliminated points with higher branching ratios. The benchmark in Table
6c
yields
BR(\mu\rightarrow e\gamma)\simeq 2.0\times 10^{-13}
. These effects are detectable with the MEG II update
[
121
]
, which is expected to improve the current sensitivity of MEG by an order of magnitude.
For the loop-stable numerical examples in this case, the following limits are obtained:
m_{\beta}<15
meV,
|m_{\beta\beta}|<27
meV, and
|(KX^{\dagger}){e4}|^{2}\lesssim 0.02
. While KATRIN aims to improve the current limit on
m\beta
to
0.2
eV, the next generation of
(\beta\beta){0\nu}
-decay experiments
[
122
]
may be able to probe values of
|m{\beta\beta}|\gtrsim 10^{-2}
eV.

Concerning the prospect of detecting the heavy neutrino pair in future collider searches, the review
[
123
]
is recommended. If the heavy neutrino pair has a mass in the
1-100
GeV range and is sufficiently long-lived, it could potentially produce displaced vertex signatures
[
124
,
125
,
126
,
127
,
128
,
129
,
130
,
131
,
132
,
133
,
134
,
135
]
and resolvable neutrino-antineutrino oscillations at colliders
[
136
]
.
Finally, the pseudo-Dirac pair of case
II
might play a role
in explaining the baryon asymmetry of the Universe
through resonant leptogenesis
[
137
,
138
]
.

However, one should take into account the washout from the interactions of the lighter sterile neutrino
species and to reconcile the light sterile neutrino paradigm with cosmology, it may be necessary to consider non-standard interactions.

The numerical examples presented are only for illustration purposes, but they support the claim that models with an approximate lepton number symmetry and an eV-scale sterile neutrino mass are viable and may contribute to the explanation of SBL anomalies.

2.3

General Remarks

2.3.1

On CP Violating Phases

In table
2.5
, the physical parameters of the 3
\nu
SM are summarised. It is important to parametrise all 6 phases.
A general parameterisation for a
3\times 3
complex orthogonal
O_{c}
is given by
[
96
,
139
]

O_{c}=\pm\penalty\ O_{c}^{23}O_{c}^{13}O_{c}^{12}\penalty\ ,

(2.77)

where, for instance,

O_{c}^{23}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&\cos{(r_{23}e^{if_{23}})}&\sin{(r_{23}e^{if_{23}})}\\
0&-\sin{(r_{23}e^{if_{23}})}&\cos{(r_{23}e^{if_{23}})}\end{pmatrix}

(2.78)

and
O_{c}^{12}
,
O_{c}^{13}
have an obviously similar definition.

U_{K}
has a well-known definition
[
3
]
, as the
3\times 3
unitary part of the leptonic mixing matrix, equivalent to the parameterisation of
U_{\textrm{PMNS}}
in eq. (
2.1
).
It is important to note that, eq. (
2.27
) can be rewritten as

K=(\mathds{1}_{3}-\eta)U_{K}=U_{K}\penalty\ H_{R}\penalty\ ,

(2.79)

the unitary part on both decompositions is equal and the hermitian parts are related by the equation

H_{R}=(\mathds{1}_{3}+X^{\dagger}X)^{-1/2}=U_{K}^{\dagger}\penalty\ (\mathds{1}_{3}-\eta)U_{K}\penalty\ ,

(2.80)

by the polar decomposition theorem. Due to the rephasing of the charged leptons, 3 phases can be removed from the left of
K
. The phases of
U_{K}
are named the
unitary phases
of the mixing matrix:
\delta
and the Majorana phases
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
, defined in eq. (
2.1
).
The phases of
H_{R}
,
p_{ij}
, are named the
hermitian phases
of the mixing matrix, or the phases of the deviations from unitarity.

H_{R}=\begin{pmatrix}h_{11}&h_{12}e^{ip_{12}}&h_{13}e^{ip_{13}}\\
h_{12}e^{-ip_{12}}&h_{22}&h_{23}e^{ip_{23}}\\
h_{13}e^{-ip_{13}}&h_{23}e^{-ip_{23}}&h_{33}\\
\end{pmatrix}\penalty\ ,

(2.81)

where
p_{ij}
are linear functions of the
O_{c}
phases
f_{ij}
. This means that if all
f_{ij}
can be written as
k\pi
then the same can be done for
p_{ij}
, with
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. In the literature, the unitary phases are also known as low-energy phases and the hermitian phases as high-energy phases
[
140
,
141
,
142
,
143
]
. This is because if deviations from
3\times 3
unitarity are negligible the hermitian phases are essentially impossible to measure at low energy, and only have effects at high energy, possibly being the source of leptogenesis.

Thus, the 6 physical phases in the
3\nu SM
are controlled by
\delta
,
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
- the phases of
U_{K}
and
f_{12}
,
f_{13}
and
f_{23}
- the phases of
O_{c}
(
p_{12}
,
p_{13}
and
p_{23}
- the phases of
H_{R}
).

2.3.2

On the Parameter Space

The parameterisation used in this study was developed in Ref.
[
30
]
for heavy neutrinos with TeV-scale masses, in the
quasi-decoupled
regime. This is distinct from the
decoupled/seesaw
regime, which is more commonly studied in the literature and where heavy neutrinos are entirely decoupled from light ones, and deviations from
3\times 3
unitarity are negligible. This work demonstrates that this parameterisation can also be applied to other spectra of heavy neutrinos, including
non-decoupled
regimes with heavy almost-sterile neutrinos with masses close to the light ones. Unlike other parameterisations used in the literature, this one can be used for this purpose without significant numerical deviations from the exact result. In this discussion,
m_{i}
refers to the masses of light neutrinos and
M_{j}
refers to the masses of heavy neutrinos.

Regarding its flexibility, in section
2.1
it was discussed the quasi-decoupled case where
m_{i}/M_{j}\ll 1
, and from Eq.(
2.18
), it is clear that
X
can only have significant entries and deviations from unitarity if
O_{c}
also has considerable (
\gg 1
) entries. On the other hand, for the Dirac matrix given in Eq.(
2.35
), and considering the perturbativity condition
m_{ij}\lesssim m_{t}
, the size of the product
O_{c}^{\dagger}\sqrt{D}
is limited, which can counterbalance the suppression from
\sqrt{d}
.

Using the same logic for the
decoupled/seesaw
regime, where heavy neutrino masses are near the GUT scale, one can observe that
X
can only have significant entries if
O_{c}
also has significant entries. However, achieving an
X
with substantial entries in this regime would require entries in
O_{c}
that are not compatible with perturbative entries in
m
generated by electroweak symmetry breaking (EWSB). As a result,
O_{c}
must have entries of
\mathcal{O}(1)
, which results in small entries for
X
and small deviations from unitarity.
The non-decoupled regimes discussed in section
2.2
present similarities to both the aforementioned regimes.

In those cases, when the complex orthogonal matrix
O_{c}
has entries of order
\mathcal{O}(1)
(which is "small" for such a matrix), it allows for
X
to have significant entries, leading to connections with short-baseline anomalies. This is because some of the
{m_{i}}/{M_{j}}
can be around
\mathcal{O}(10^{-2}\text{--}10^{-3})
when
M_{1(,2)}\sim\text{eV}
, as shown in Eq. (
2.18
).
However, embedding this eV neutrino in a type-I seesaw with 3 sterile neutrinos and with reasonable Yukawa couplings
m_{ij}
of EWSB origin,
m_{ij}\sim\mathcal{O}(1-0.01)\,m_{t}
, while keeping keeping
X
sizeable, leads to non-trivial constraints on
O_{c}
.
In particular,
spectra where the highest heavy neutrino(s) mass(es) is (are) not extremely large (e.g. at the GeV – TeV scale) will need an
O_{c}
with some sizeable entries like in the quasi-decoupled regime. On the other hand, spectra where one of the
M_{j}
is near the GUT scale require an
O_{c}
with
\mathcal{O}(1)
entries, like in the decoupled regime. These requirements on the size of some entries of
O_{c}
are necessary to achieve reasonable Yukawa couplings.

Other parameterisations in the literature, such as those in
[
96
,
52
]
, also begin with an exact parameterisation of
\mathcal{V}
. However, these approaches define the mixing matrix using an infinite series in a matrix that controls the deviations from unitarity. To perform explicit calculations, a truncated series is necessary, leading to a good approximation only for
M_{j}>100
GeV, as discussed in section
2.1
, and resulting in small deviations from unitarity. This parameterisation is not suitable for scenarios in which deviations from unitarity are significant, as in the case of a non-decoupled sterile neutrino sector. The parameterisation used in this work, however, is appropriate for such scenarios and may provide a direct connection to symmetries in an underlying model.

Figure
2.5
is an illustration of the whole parameter space of the
n_{R}\nu
SM. It is a density plot of the determinant of
d
, hence the darkest the blue the higher the value of

det(d)=m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}\penalty\ .

(2.82)

In the
x
-axis one can find

det(d_{X}^{2})=(d_{X}^{1}d_{X}^{2}d_{X}^{3})^{2}\penalty\ ,

(2.83)

where
(d_{X}^{i})^{2}
are the eigenvalues of
X^{\dagger}X
and
d_{X}^{2}
a diagonal matrix with them in the diagonal in increasing order. Hence,
det(d_{X}^{2})
is a good theoretical measure of the size of deviations from
3\times 3
unitarity. Note that due to the properties of orthogonal complex matrices, see. eq. (
2.18
), one will always have
(d_{X}^{3})^{2}\gg(d_{X}^{2})^{2}>(d_{X}^{1})^{2}
.

As for the
y
-axis one has

det(D)=M_{1}M_{2}M_{3}\penalty\ .

(2.84)

Figure 2.5
:
Projection of the parameter space of the
n_{R}\nu
SM in the deviations from unitarity - heavy neutrino masses space. The pink and orange lines are just placeholders to depict the log scale used and to show how fast
m_{1}
approaches zero for lighter tones of blue.

It is easy to distinguish the vanilla seesaw region - green symbols - at high
det(D)
and low
det(d_{X}^{2})
, the quasi-decoupled region, in the middle range of
det(D)
and
det(d_{X}^{2})
and the non-decoupled region - at low
det(D)
and high
det(d_{X}^{2})
.

The red line corresponds to the value of
det(d)
for which the sum of all light neutrino masses matches the bound from Planck
[
144
]
, one of the most robust bounds
[
3
]
. This can be deducted because one can write the light neutrino masses as
[
3
]

m_{1}=\sqrt{\Delta m_{31}^{2}z}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ m_{2}=\sqrt{\Delta m_{31}^{2}(z+a)}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ m_{3}=\sqrt{\Delta m_{31}^{2}(z+1)}

(2.85)

where

\Delta m_{ij}^{2}=m_{i}^{2}-m_{j}^{2}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ a=\frac{\Delta m_{21}^{2}}{\Delta m_{31}^{2}}\penalty\ ,

(2.86)

with
z
being a free parameter. The current best-fit values for normal ordering are

\Delta m_{21}^{2}=7.50\times 10^{-5}\penalty\ \textrm{eV}^{2}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \Delta m_{31}^{2}=2.55\times 10^{-3}\textrm{eV}^{2}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ a\approx 0.02941\penalty\ .

(2.87)

Thus, using these experimental values and the bound from PLANCK
[
144
]
, one can solve numerically for
z
and obtain a value for
det(d)
, which is only a function of
z
, as the
m_{i}
, when the
\Delta m_{ij}^{2}
are fixed

m_{1}(z)+m_{2}(z)+m_{3}(z)=0.12\penalty\ \textrm{eV}\implies z\approx 0.355\implies det(d)\approx 5.54\times 10^{-5}\penalty\ \textrm{eV}^{3}\penalty\ .

(2.88)

Hence, values of
z
larger than
0.355
, which translates into

m_{1}>0.0301\penalty\ \textrm{eV}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ m_{2}>0.0313\penalty\ \textrm{eV}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ m_{3}>0.0588\penalty\ \textrm{eV}\penalty\ ,

(2.89)

are ruled out.
It’s interesting to note that most of this ruled out region, above the red line, would be ruled out due to perturbativity. This is because most of the area of the forbidden region is for high values of
det(D)
and high values of
det(d_{X}^{2})
. From eq. (
2.17
) it is easy to see that

det(m)\sim det(X^{\dagger})\penalty\ det(D)\penalty\ ,

(2.90)

and too large values of
det(D)
and
det(d_{X}^{2})
can easily yield non-perturbative Yukawa couplings.
Before proceeding to another section, it is relevant to discuss how models with very light neutrino masses,
z\ll 1
, are still allowed, although all the points drawn in the plot, taken from published papers, are for higher values of
m_{1}
.
From eq. (
2.18
) one can understand that very small light neutrino masses (
m_{1}\rightarrow 0
) introduces an extra suppression factor on
X
, beyond the usual
D^{-1/2}
. Nonetheless, although more challenging, it is still possible to have sizeable deviations from unitarity, by choosing an appropriate
O_{c}
, with large enough eigenvalues.

Chapter 3

The Standard Model +
n_{u}
up-type vector-like quarks +
n_{d}
down type vector-like quarks (
n_{u}
SM
n_{d}
)

Vector-like quarks are quarks where the right-handed and the left-handed components transform
in the same way under the gauge group. As a consequence of this, all their interactions are vector-like or, in other words, not “chiral”. That is, wherever the left-handed component of the VLQ can be introduced in the Lagrangian, the same can be done for the right-handed component. This does not happen for the SM fields, since left-handed components are part of a
SU(2)
doublet and right-handed components are
SU(2)
singlets. This particularity of VLQs allows the existence of bare mass-terms, meaning that the VLQ mass might have an origin beyond the Higgs mechanism. Hence, their masses can be much larger than the Higgs vev, while still leaving considerable traces at low-energy.

There are only seven different types of VLQ multiplets which may mix with the SM quarks through Yukawa couplings with the Higgs doublet
[
145
,
146
]
.
They consist of isosinglets, doublets or triplets, all coloured in the fundamental representation. The complete quantum numbers are listed in Table
3.1
.

Multiplet

U

D

\begin{pmatrix}U\cr D\end{pmatrix}

\begin{pmatrix}X\cr U\end{pmatrix}

\begin{pmatrix}D\cr Y\end{pmatrix}

\begin{pmatrix}X\cr U\cr D\end{pmatrix}

\begin{pmatrix}U\cr D\cr Y\end{pmatrix}

SU(2)_{L}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{2}

\mathbf{2}

\mathbf{2}

\mathbf{3}

\mathbf{3}

U(1)_{Y}

2/3

-1/3

1/6

7/6

-5/6

2/3

-1/3

Table 3.1
:

The only VLQ multiplets that couple to the SM quarks through Yukawa interactions (
Q=I_{3}+Y
).

This thesis will only cover extensions of the quark sector with vector-like singlet quarks
U
and
D
, often denoted in the literature as
T
and
B
, respectively. Thus, in the context of this thesis, the names isosinglet vector-like quarks and vector-like quarks will be used interchangeably.

In section
3.1
of this chapter a model where
n_{u}
isosinglet vector-like up-type (
Q=+2/3
) quarks and
n_{d}
isosinglet vector-like down-type (
Q=-1/3
) quarks are added to the SM, without imposing any symmetry, is discussed. In section
3.2.1
(
3.2.2
) a model where only
n_{u}
(
n_{d}
) isosinglet vector-like up-type (down-type) quarks are added to the SM, without imposing any symmetry, will be covered.
These extension of the SM will be treated exactly using the parameterisation first devised in
[
30
]
, in the more succinct form of
[
32
]
and
[
33
]
.

3.1

The Most General Case (
n_{u}
SM
n_{d}
)

To begin, it is important to establish the notation for the most general case - the scenario involving
n_{u}
up (
U^{0}_{Lr}
,
U^{0}_{Rr}
) and
n_{d}
down (
D^{0}_{Ls}
,
D^{0}_{Rs}
) vector-like isosinglet quarks. Here, the flavour indices
r
and
s
range from 1 to
n_{u}
and
n_{d}
respectively, and the superscript "0" denotes the flavour basis.
Once these vector-like quarks are introduced, the Yukawa interactions in this basis are given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{\text{Y}}\,=\,-\,\Big[&\,\,\overline{Q}_{Li}^{0}\,\tilde{\Phi}\,\left(Y_{u}\right)_{ij}\,u^{0}_{Rj}\,+\,\overline{Q}_{Li}^{0}\,\tilde{\Phi}\,\left(\overline{Y}\!_{u}\right)_{is}\,U^{0}_{Rs}\\
+&\,\,\overline{Q}_{Li}^{0}\,\Phi\,\left(Y_{d}\right)_{ij}\,d^{0}_{Rj}\,+\,\overline{Q}_{Li}^{0}\,\Phi\,\left(\overline{Y}\!_{d}\right)_{ir}\,D^{0}_{Rr}\,\Big]+\text{h.c.}\,,\end{split}

(3.1)

where the indices
i
and
j
run from 1 to 3 as in the SM and
Y_{d}
,
Y_{u}
are the SM Yukawa couplings. Here,
Q_{L}=(u_{L}\,\,\,d_{L})^{T}
are the SM quark doublets,
u_{R}
and
d_{R}
are the SM quark singlets,
\Phi
is the Higgs doublet and
\tilde{\Phi}=i\tau_{2}\Phi^{*}
is its
SU(2)_{L}
conjugate. The matrices
{\overline{Y}\!_{d}}
and
{\overline{Y}\!_{u}}
denote the new Yukawa couplings to the extra right-handed fields.

Since the VLQs are
SU(2)
singlets, the ensuing mass terms are, in general, allowed and must therefore be included

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{\text{b.m.}}\,=\,-\,\Big[&\,\,\overline{U}^{0}_{Lr}\,\left(\,\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{u}\right)_{ri}\,u^{0}_{Ri}\,+\,\overline{U}^{0}_{Lr}\,\left(M_{u}\right)_{rr^{\prime}}\,U^{0}_{Rr^{\prime}}\\
+&\,\,\overline{D}^{0}_{Ls}\,\left(\,\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{d}\right)_{si}\,d^{0}_{Ri}\,+\,\overline{D}^{0}_{Ls}\,\left(M_{d}\right)_{ss^{\prime}}\,D^{0}_{Rs^{\prime}}\,\Big]+\text{h.c.}\,,\end{split}

(3.2)

where
\,\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{q}
and
M_{q}
(
q=u,d
) are, respectively,
n_{q}\times 3
and
n_{q}\times n_{q}
general complex matrices. At this stage, these terms are interpreted as bare mass (b.m.) terms. In certain models, they may arise from the vacuum expectation value (VEV) of a complex scalar singlet, for instance.

After SSB, one has

\Phi=\big(0\,\,\,\frac{v+h}{\sqrt{2}}\big)^{T}

in the unitary gauge, where
h
is the Higgs field and
v\simeq 246\;\mathrm{GeV}
.
Additional mass terms are generated from
\mathcal{L}_{\text{Y}}
of (
3.1
). All mass terms can be collected in a compact form,

\mathcal{L}_{\text{M}}\,=\,-\begin{pmatrix}\overline{d}^{0}_{L}&\overline{D}^{0}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\,\mathcal{M}_{d}\,\begin{pmatrix}d^{0}_{R}\\[2.84526pt]
D^{0}_{R}\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}\overline{u}^{0}_{L}&\overline{U}^{0}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\,\mathcal{M}_{u}\,\begin{pmatrix}u^{0}_{R}\\[2.84526pt]
U^{0}_{R}\end{pmatrix}+\text{h.c.}\,,

(3.3)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{q}\,=\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c;{2pt/2pt}c}\,m_{q}&\,\lx@text@overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_q\\[4.2679pt]
\hline\cr[2pt/2pt]\!{\,\,\,\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{q}\,\,\,}&\!{\,\,\,M_q\,\,\,\\[2.84526pt]
\end{array}\right)\,,}

with
m_{q}=\frac{v}{\sqrt{2}}Y_{q}
,
\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}=\frac{v}{\sqrt{2}}\overline{Y}\!_{q}
and
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{q}
and
M_{q}
defined in eq. (
3.2
).
It is important to note that, generally speaking, the matrices
\mathcal{M}_{q}
aren’t symmetric or Hermitian. Due to their different origin, a hierarchy
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{q}\sim M_{q}\gg\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}\sim m_{q}
is expected.
Whenever there’s no potential for confusion, the indices
q=u,d
will be omitted.
The mass matrices
\mathcal{M}_{q}
can be diagonalized by biunitary transformations — their singular value decompositions — of the form

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}^{q}_{L}}^{\dagger}\,\,\mathcal{M}_{q}\,\,\mathcal{V}_{R}^{q}\,=\,\mathcal{D}_{q}\,,\quad\text{with }\mathcal{D}_{q}\,=\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c;{2pt/2pt}c}\,\,\,d_{q}&\,\,\,0\\[4.2679pt]
\hline\cr[2pt/2pt]\!\mathrlap{\smash{\underbrace{\phantom{\begin{matrix}\,\,\,\,0\,\,\,\,\,\end{matrix}}}_{\mbox{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}}\,\,\,\,0&\mathrlap{\smash{\underbrace{\phantom{\begin{matrix}\,\,\,\,D_{q}\,\,\,\, \end{matrix}}}_{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\,\,\,n_{q}$}}}}\,\,\,\,D_q\\[2.84526pt]
\end{array}\right)\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{l}\left.\vphantom{\begin{matrix}0\end{matrix}}\right\}\scriptstyle\,3\\[0.0pt]
\left.\vphantom{\begin{matrix}D\end{matrix}}\right\}\scriptstyle\,n_{q}\end{array}\,,

where the unitary rotations
\mathcal{V}_{L,R}^{q}
connect the flavour and physical bases by using four rotations (two for each sector).
The diagonal matrices
d_{q}
and
D_{q}
contain the light
(d_{q})_{i}\geq 0
(
i=1,2,3
) and heavy
(D_{q})_{r}>0
(
r=1,\ldots,n_{q}
) quark masses. It is important to note that
D_{q}
is a matrix, not a down-type quark field. The notations
d_{u}=\diag(m_{u},m_{c},m_{t})
,
d_{d}=\diag(m_{d},m_{s},m_{b})
,
D_{u}=\diag(m_{U1},\ldots,m_{Un_{u}})
, and
D_{d}=\diag(m_{D1},\ldots,m_{Dn_{d}})
will be used whenever there is no potential for confusion.
Regardless of how one parameterises the four diagonalization matrices
\mathcal{V}^{q}_{\chi}
(
q=u,d
;
\chi=L,R
), it is useful to divide them into two blocks,

\mathcal{V}^{q}_{\chi}\,=\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\\[-11.38109pt]
\qquad A^{q}_{\chi}\\[5.69054pt]
\hline\cr[2pt/2pt]\\[-11.38109pt]
\qquad B^{q}_{\chi}\\[5.69054pt]
\end{array}\right)\begin{array}[]{l}\left.\vphantom{\begin{matrix}A\end{matrix}}\right\}\scriptstyle\,3\\[0.0pt]
\left.\vphantom{\begin{matrix}B\end{matrix}}\right\}\scriptstyle\,n_{q}\end{array}\,,

(3.10)

where
A^{q}_{\chi}
is a
3\times(3+n_{q})
matrix and
B^{q}_{\chi}
is an
n_{q}\times(3+n_{q})
matrix.
The flavour basis and the physical basis (no ‘0’ superscript) are thus connected via

\begin{pmatrix}\,u^{0}_{\chi}\,\\[2.84526pt]
U^{0}_{\chi}\end{pmatrix}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}\,A^{u}_{\chi}\,\\[2.84526pt]
B^{u}_{\chi}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\,u_{\chi}\,\\[2.84526pt]
U_{\chi}\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad\begin{pmatrix}\,d^{0}_{\chi}\,\\[2.84526pt]
D^{0}_{\chi}\end{pmatrix}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}\,A^{d}_{\chi}\,\\[2.84526pt]
B^{d}_{\chi}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\,d_{\chi}\,\\[2.84526pt]
D_{\chi}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(3.11)

with
\chi=L,R
.

Within each sector, using the diagonalization relation eq. (
3.1
), one can write the mass matrix blocks of eq. (
3.1
) in terms of the physical masses as

\displaystyle m_{q}

\displaystyle=A^{q}_{L}\,\mathcal{D}_{q}\,{A_{R}^{q}}^{\dagger}\,,\quad\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}=A^{q}_{L}\,\mathcal{D}_{q}\,{B_{R}^{q}}^{\dagger}\,,

(3.12)

\displaystyle\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{q}

\displaystyle=B^{q}_{L}\,\mathcal{D}_{q}\,{A_{R}^{q}}^{\dagger}\,,\quad M_{q}=B^{q}_{L}\,\mathcal{D}_{q}\,{B_{R}^{q}}^{\dagger}\,.

Finally, unitarity of the
\mathcal{V}^{q}_{\chi}
implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{q}_{\chi}\penalty\ \mathcal{V}^{q{\dagger}}_{\chi}=\begin{pmatrix}\,A^{q}_{\chi}\,\\[5.69054pt]
B^{q}_{\chi}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\,{A^{q}_{\chi}}^{\dagger}&{B^{q}_{\chi}}^{\dagger}\,\end{pmatrix}\,

\displaystyle=\,\begin{pmatrix}\,A^{q}_{\chi}\,{A^{q}_{\chi}}^{\dagger}&A^{q}_{\chi}\,{B^{q}_{\chi}}^{\dagger}\,\\[5.69054pt]
\,B^{q}_{\chi}\,{A^{q}_{\chi}}^{\dagger}&B^{q}_{\chi}\,{B^{q}_{\chi}}^{\dagger}\,\end{pmatrix}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}\,\mathds{1}_{3}\,&\,0\,\\[5.69054pt]
\,0\,&\,\mathds{1}_{n_{q}}\,\end{pmatrix}\,,

(3.13a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{q{\dagger}}_{\chi}\penalty\ \mathcal{V}^{q}_{\chi}=\begin{pmatrix}\,{A^{q}_{\chi}}^{\dagger}&{B^{q}_{\chi}}^{\dagger}\,\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\,A^{q}_{\chi}\,\\[5.69054pt]
B^{q}_{\chi}\end{pmatrix}\,

\displaystyle=\,\,{A^{q}_{\chi}}^{\dagger}\,A^{q}_{\chi}+{B^{q}_{\chi}}^{\dagger}\,B^{q}_{\chi}\,\,=\,\,\mathds{1}_{3+n_{q}}\,,

(3.13b)

for each
q=u,d
and
\chi=L,R
.

Electromagnetic interactions of quarks, including the VLQ isosinglets, are described by the Lagrangian

\mathcal{L}_{A}=-e\,J^{\mu}_{\text{em}}A_{\mu}\,,

(3.14)

with

\displaystyle J^{\mu}_{\text{em}}

\displaystyle\equiv\,\frac{2}{3}\Big(\overline{u_{i}^{0}}\gamma^{\mu}{u_{i}^{0}}+\overline{U_{r}^{0}}\gamma^{\mu}{U_{r}^{0}}\Big)-\frac{1}{3}\Big(\overline{d_{i}^{0}}\gamma^{\mu}{d_{i}^{0}}+\overline{D_{s}^{0}}\gamma^{\mu}{D_{s}^{0}}\Big)

(3.15)

\displaystyle=\,\frac{2}{3}\Big(\overline{u_{i}}\gamma^{\mu}{u_{i}}+\overline{U_{r}}\gamma^{\mu}{U_{r}}\Big)-\frac{1}{3}\Big(\overline{d_{i}}\gamma^{\mu}{d_{i}}+\overline{D_{s}}\gamma^{\mu}{D_{s}}\Big)\,,

where
\psi\equiv\psi_{L}+\psi_{R}
for
\psi\in\{u_{i}^{(0)},d_{i}^{(0)},U_{r}^{(0)},D_{s}^{(0)}\}
. As before, the index
i
runs from 1 to 3 as in the SM, while the indices
r,s
run from 1 to
n_{u,d}
. Note that the structure of the electromagnetic current
J^{\mu}_{\text{em}}
is unchanged in going from the flavour to the mass basis.

The charged current Lagrangian for the quarks in the mass basis is given by

\mathcal{L}_{W}^{q}=-\frac{g}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\overline{u}_{L}&\overline{U}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\,V\,\gamma^{\mu}\begin{pmatrix}d_{L}\\[5.69054pt]
D_{L}\end{pmatrix}W_{\mu}^{+}\,+\,\text{h.c.}\penalty\ ,

(3.16)

where

V=A_{L}^{u^{\dagger}}A_{L}^{d}\penalty\ ,

(3.17)

is the
(3+n_{u})\times(3+n_{d})
non-unitary mixing matrix. The mixing matrix
V
, which may have a rectangular shape when
n_{u}\neq n_{d}
, is typically not unitary. The CKM quark mixing matrix,
V_{\textrm{CKM}}
, is the upper-left
3\times 3
block of
V
, also not unitary in general.

The weak neutral current Lagrangian in the mass basis is given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{Z}^{q}&=-\frac{g}{2\cos{\theta_{W}}}Z_{\mu}\left[\begin{pmatrix}\overline{u}_{L}&\overline{U}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\,F^{u}\,\gamma^{\mu}\begin{pmatrix}u_{L}\\[5.69054pt]
U_{L}\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}\overline{d}_{L}&\overline{D}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\,F^{d}\,\gamma^{\mu}\begin{pmatrix}d_{L}\\[5.69054pt]
D_{L}\end{pmatrix}-2\sin^{2}{\theta_{W}}J^{\mu\penalty\ q}_{\text{em}}\right]\,,\end{split}

(3.18)

while the part of the Lagrangian that contains the Higgs interactions with the quarks in the mass basis is given by

\mathcal{L}_{H}^{q}\,=\,-\frac{h}{v}\bigg[\begin{pmatrix}\overline{u}_{L}&\overline{U}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\,F^{u}\,\mathcal{D}_{u}\begin{pmatrix}u_{R}\\[5.69054pt]
U_{R}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\overline{d}_{L}&\overline{D}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\,F^{d}\,\mathcal{D}_{d}\begin{pmatrix}d_{R}\\[5.69054pt]
D_{R}\end{pmatrix}\bigg]+\,\text{h.c.}\,\penalty\ ,

(3.19)

where

F^{q}=A_{L}^{q^{\dagger}}A_{L}^{q}\penalty\ .

(3.20)

Note that the
F^{q}
are present in interactions with the Higgs and the Z boson and are the matrices that controls Flavour Changing Neutral Couplings (FCNC). This might become clearer if these are written as

\displaystyle F^{u}

\displaystyle\equiv\,{A_{L}^{u}}^{\dagger}A_{L}^{u}\,=\,\mathds{1}-{B_{L}^{u}}^{\dagger}B_{L}^{u}\,=\,VV^{\dagger}\,,

(3.21)

\displaystyle F^{d}

\displaystyle\equiv\,{A_{L}^{d}}^{\dagger}A_{L}^{d}\,=\,\mathds{1}-{B_{L}^{d}}^{\dagger}B_{L}^{d}\,=\,V^{\dagger}V\,.

The strength of FCNC is proportional to the off-diagonal elements of
F^{u,d}
, and eq. (
3.21
) shows that these come from the deviations from the identity matrix of
F^{q}
. This also shows that there is a strong connection between the strength of FCNC and deviations of
V
from unitarity.

It should be noted that
F^{u,d}
matrices are in general not equal to the identity matrix, which leads to the occurrence of FCNC processes mediated by both the Higgs boson and the
Z
boson. The strength of Higgs-mediated FCNC processes is determined by the off-diagonal elements of
F^{u,d}
matrices as well as the ratios of the diagonal elements of
\mathcal{D}_{u,d}
to the vacuum expectation value
v
. This is in contrast to the case of
Z
-mediated FCNC processes. Because of this, in the Higgs sector, for transitions involving only the lighter quarks
u
and
c
, there is a suppression factor of
m_{u}/v\sim 10^{-5}
or
m_{c}/v\sim 10^{-2}
.

The same steps performed in the beginning of chapter
2
can be done for the quark sector, allowing one to write the quark form of eq. (
2
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\chi}^{q}\,=\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c;{2pt/2pt}c}\,\,\,K_{\chi}^{q}&\,\,\,K_\chi^q\,{X_\chi^q}^\textdagger\\[4.2679pt]
\hline\cr[2pt/2pt]{-\overline{\!K}\,_{\!\chi}^{\!q}\,X_{\chi}^{q}\,\,}&\quad\,\,\lx@text@overline{\!K}\,_{\!\chi}^{\!q}\quad\,\,\\[2.84526pt]
\end{array}\right)\penalty\ .

Eq. (
3.1
) gives that the
(3+n_{q})\times(3+n_{q})
unitary matrices
\mathcal{V}^{q}_{\chi}
introduced in eq. (
3.1
) and eq. (
3.10
) can be written, without loss of generality, using a non-singular
3\times 3
general complex matrix,
K^{q}_{\chi}
, a non-singular
n_{q}\times n_{q}
general complex matrix
\overline{\!K}\,^{q}_{\chi}
and a
n_{q}\times 3
matrix,
X^{q}_{\chi}
. As in chapter
2
, from eq. (
3.10
) one obtains

A^{q}_{\chi}=(K^{q}_{\chi}\penalty\ \penalty\ K^{q}_{\chi}{X^{q}_{\chi}}^{\dagger})\penalty\ ,\penalty\ B^{q}_{\chi}=(-\overline{\!K}\,^{q}_{\chi}X^{q}_{\chi}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \overline{\!K}\,^{q}_{\chi})\penalty\ ,

(3.24)

Using eq. (
3.24
) and the unitarity relations in eqs. (
3.13a
) and (
3.13b
), eq. (
3.12
) becomes

\begin{split}&m_{q}\,=\,K_{L}^{q}\left(d_{q}+{X_{L}^{q}}^{\dagger}\,D_{q}\,X_{R}^{q}\right){K_{R}^{q}}^{\dagger},\\
&\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}\,=\,K_{L}^{q}\left({X_{L}^{q}}^{\dagger}\,D_{q}-d_{q}\,{X_{R}^{q}}^{\dagger}\right){\overline{\!K}\,_{R}^{q}}^{\dagger},\\
&\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{q}\,=\,\overline{\!K}\,_{L}^{q}\left(D_{q}\,X_{R}^{q}-X_{L}^{q}\,d_{q}\right){K_{R}^{q}}^{\dagger},\\
&M_{q}\,=\,\overline{\!K}\,_{L}^{q}\left(D_{q}+X_{L}^{q}\,d_{q}\,{X_{R}^{q}}^{\dagger}\right){\overline{\!K}\,_{R}^{q}}^{\dagger},\end{split}

(3.25)

where
m_{q}
is the
3\times 3
Dirac mass matrix for the quarks and the entries of the
3\times n_{q}
matrix,
\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}
, are proportional to the Higgs vacuum expectation value, as well. The
n_{q}\times 3
mass matrix,
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{q}
, and the
n_{q}\times n_{q}
mass matrix,
M_{q}
, are bare mass terms involving only singlet quark fields.

It is always possible to go to a WB where
\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}
is
0
, also for
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{q}
, see section 3.2.1 of ref.
[
33
]
. In the vanishing
\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}
WB, one can proceed like in the neutrino case to obtain a formula for
X_{\chi}^{q}
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &X_{L}^{q}=\sqrt{D_{q}^{-1}}P^{q}\sqrt{d_{q}}\penalty\ ,\\
&X_{R}^{q}=\sqrt{D_{q}}P^{q}\sqrt{d_{q}^{-1}}\penalty\ ,\end{aligned}

(3.26)

where
P^{q}
is a general complex matrix and
d_{q}
(
D_{q}
) is a diagonal matrix with the masses of the light (heavy) quarks in the diagonal.
A keen reader will notice that eq. (
3.26
) amounts to nothing, since
P^{q}
are general complex matrices, so will
X_{L}^{q}
and
X_{R}^{q}
be.
X_{L}^{q}
and
X_{R}^{q}
are completely general complex matrices which only need to satisfy

\displaystyle d_{q}X_{L}^{q\dagger}

\displaystyle=X_{R}^{q\dagger}D_{q}\quad\text{in the vanishing }\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{q}\text{ WB}\,,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad d_{q}X_{R}^{q\dagger}

\displaystyle=X_{L}^{q\dagger}D_{q}\quad\text{in the vanishing }\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}\text{ WB}\,.

(3.28)

X_{L}^{q}
will, as in the neutrino sector, control the size of the deviations from unitarity of the
(3+n_{u})\times(3+n_{d})
non-unitary mixing matrix
V
.
Naturally, its biggest constraint will be how large one wants these deviations to be. A very important constraint will also be the perturbativity of the theory. More on that in section
3.1.2
.
The following definitions can also be obtained from the unitarity relations in eqs. (
3.13a
) and (
3.13b
),

\begin{split}&K^{q}_{\chi}=U^{q}_{K\chi}(\mathds{1}_{3}+{X_{\chi}^{q}}^{\dagger}X_{\chi}^{q})^{-1/2}\,,\\
&\overline{\!K}\,^{q}_{\chi}=U^{q}_{\overline{\!K}\,\chi}(\mathds{1}_{n_{q}}+X_{\chi}^{q}{X_{\chi}^{q}}^{\dagger})^{-1/2}\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(3.29)

where
U^{q}_{K\chi}
and
U^{q}_{\overline{\!K}\,\chi}
are unitary matrices, since the unitarity relations only define
K

and
\overline{\!K}\,
up to a unitary matrix on the left. The combination
{K_{L}^{u}}^{\dagger}K_{L}^{d}
will play the role of the CKM mixing matrix and is only unitary when
X_{L}^{u},X_{L}^{d}\xrightarrow{}\textbf{0}
,

V_{\textrm{CKM}}={K_{L}^{u}}^{\dagger}K_{L}^{d}\penalty\ .

(3.30)

The
(3+n_{u})\times(3+n_{d})
non-unitary mixing matrix
V
, first defined in eq. (
3.17
), can now be written as

V=A^{u\dagger}_{L}A^{d}_{L}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c;{2pt/2pt}c}\,\,\,V_{\text{CKM}}&\,\,\,V_\text{CKM}\,{X_L^{d \dagger}\\[4.2679pt]
\hline\cr[2pt/2pt]\!\!\mathrlap{\smash{\underbrace{\phantom{\begin{matrix}\,\,\,\,{X_{L}^{u}}\,V_{\text{CKM}}\,\,\,\,\,\end{matrix}}}_{\mbox{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}}\,\,\,\,{X_{L}^{u}}\,V_{\text{CKM}}&\!\mathrlap{\smash{\underbrace{\phantom{\begin{matrix}\,\,\,{X_{L}^{u}} \,V_{\text{CKM}}\,{X_{L}^{d}}^{\textdagger}\,\,\,\, \end{matrix}}}_{\mbox{$\scriptstyle\,\,\,n_{d}$}}}}\,\,\,{X_L^u} \,V_\text{CKM}\,{X_L^d}^\textdagger\\[2.84526pt]
\end{array}\right)\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{l}\left.\vphantom{\begin{matrix}V_{\text{CKM}}\,{X_{L}^{d}}^{\dagger}\end{matrix}}\right\}\scriptstyle\,3\\[0.0pt]
\left.\vphantom{\begin{matrix}V_{\text{CKM}}\,{X_{L}^{d}}^{\dagger}\end{matrix}}\right\}\scriptstyle\,n_{u}\end{array}.\\
}

(3.31)

The matrices relevant for FCNC, first defined in eq. (
3.20
), can also be rewritten as

F^{q}=\begin{pmatrix}(\mathds{1}_{3}+{X_{L}^{q}}^{\dagger}X_{L}^{q})^{-1}&(\mathds{1}_{3}+{X_{L}^{q}}^{\dagger}{X_{L}^{q}})^{-1}{X_{L}^{q}}^{\dagger}\\
{X_{L}^{q}}(\mathds{1}_{3}+{X_{L}^{q}}^{\dagger}{X_{L}^{q}})^{-1}&{X_{L}^{q}}(\mathds{1}_{3}+{X_{L}^{q}}^{\dagger}{X_{L}^{q}})^{-1}{X_{L}^{q}}^{\dagger}\\
\end{pmatrix}\penalty\ ,

(3.32)

Finally, for completeness, the exact formula for the
(3+n_{q})\times(3+n_{q})
unitary matrix
\mathcal{V}^{q}_{\chi}
assumes the form

\mathcal{V}^{q}_{\chi}=\begin{pmatrix}U^{q}_{K\chi}(\mathds{1}_{3}+{X_{\chi}^{q}}^{\dagger}X_{\chi}^{q})^{-1/2}&U^{q}_{K\chi}(\mathds{1}_{3}+{X_{\chi}^{q}}^{\dagger}X_{\chi}^{q})^{-1/2}X_{\chi}^{q{\dagger}}\\
-U^{q}_{\overline{\!K}\,\chi}(\mathds{1}_{n_{q}}+X_{\chi}^{q}{X_{\chi}^{q}}^{\dagger})^{-1/2}X^{q}_{\chi}&U^{q}_{\overline{\!K}\,\chi}(\mathds{1}_{n_{q}}+X_{\chi}^{q}{X_{\chi}^{q}}^{\dagger})^{-1/2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\penalty\ .

(3.33)

3.1.1

Weak Bases and Physical Parameters in the presence of VLQs

Given that in the context of this thesis no symmetry is being assumed when adding VLQs to the SM Lagrangian, many new parameters will emerge. It is important to count them properly and to disclose which of them are physical. This can be done in a general treatment using Weak-basis (WB) transformations. Even when one is working under a model which restricts the parameter space via symmetries, the procedure and results that follows will be valid. For this discussion for the quark sector of the SM see section 3.1 of ref.
[
33
]
.

As established in eqs. (
3.1
) (
3.2
), the flavour sector of the
n_{u}
SM
n_{d}
looks like

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}\,=\,-\,\Big[&\,\,\overline{Q}_{L}^{0}\,\tilde{\Phi}\,Y_{u}\,u^{0}_{R}\,+\,\overline{Q}_{L}^{0}\,\tilde{\Phi}\,\overline{Y}\!_{u}\,U^{0}_{R}\,+\,\overline{U}^{0}_{L}\,\,\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{u}\,u^{0}_{R}\,+\,\overline{U}^{0}_{L}\,M_{u}\,U^{0}_{R}\\
+&\,\,\overline{Q}_{L}^{0}\,\Phi\,Y_{d}\,d^{0}_{R}\,+\,\overline{Q}_{L}^{0}\,\Phi\,\overline{Y}\!_{d}\,D^{0}_{R}\,+\,\overline{D}^{0}_{L}\,\,\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{d}\,d^{0}_{R}\,+\,\overline{D}^{0}_{L}\,M_{d}\,D^{0}_{R}\,\Big]+\text{h.c.}\,,\end{split}

(3.34)

where all matrices are complex.
The most general WB transformation in this context takes the form

\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}l}u^{0}_{L}\,\to\,W_{L}\,u^{0}_{L}\,,&d^{0}_{L}\,\to\,W_{L}\,d^{0}_{L}\,,\\[5.69054pt]
U^{0}_{L}\,\to\,W_{L}^{U}\,U^{0}_{L}\,,&D^{0}_{L}\,\to\,W_{L}^{D}\,D^{0}_{L}\,,\\[8.53581pt]
\begin{pmatrix}u^{0}_{R}\\[2.84526pt]
U^{0}_{R}\end{pmatrix}\,\to\,\mathcal{W}_{R}^{u}\begin{pmatrix}u^{0}_{R}\\[2.84526pt]
U^{0}_{R}\end{pmatrix}\,,&\begin{pmatrix}d^{0}_{R}\\[2.84526pt]
D^{0}_{R}\end{pmatrix}\,\to\,\mathcal{W}_{R}^{d}\begin{pmatrix}d^{0}_{R}\\[2.84526pt]
D^{0}_{R}\end{pmatrix}\,,\end{array}

(3.35)

where
W_{L}\sim 3\times 3
,
W_{L}^{U,D}\sim n_{u,d}\times n_{u,d}
and
\mathcal{W}^{u,d}_{R}\sim(3+n_{u,d})\times(3+n_{u,d})
are unitary matrices in flavour space.
It should be noted that, in a WB transformation, changes to
u_{L}^{0}
and
d_{L}^{0}
are interdependent, as in the SM case. However, this interdependence is not necessary for the transformations of the isosinglet fields
U_{L}^{0}
and
D_{L}^{0}
. Furthermore, the transformation matrices for the right-handed isosinglet fields
\mathcal{W}^{u,d}_{R}
are more extensive than those in the Standard Model. They enable mixing between
u_{R}^{0}
and
U_{R}^{0}
(and similarly for
d_{R}^{0}
and
U_{R}^{0}
), as the
3+n_{q}
right-handed quarks in each sector (
q=u,d
) carry the same quantum numbers.

While the statements above are presented for the case where
n_{f}=3
(the number of families of SM quarks), they can be readily generalized and applied to the case where
n_{f}\neq 3
.

3.1.1.1

Useful weak bases

Different WB choices allow for particular forms of the extended quark mass matrices
\mathcal{M}_{q}
, defined in eq. (
3.1
). Since the mass matrices contain contributions from both Yukawa and bare mass terms, see eqs. (
3.1
) (
3.2
), it is easier to work directly with the mass matrices in this context.
For generic
n_{u}
and
n_{d}
, under the WB change of eq. (
3.35
), the mass matrices are transformed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{u}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}m_{u}&\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{u}\\[5.69054pt]
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{u}&M_{u}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\to\quad\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{u}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}W_{L}^{\dagger}\,m_{u}&W_{L}^{\dagger}\,\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{u}\\[5.69054pt]
{W_{L}^{U}}^{\dagger}\,\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{u}&{W_{L}^{U}}^{\dagger}\,M_{u}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{W}_{R}^{u}\,,

(3.36)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{d}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}m_{d}&\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{d}\\[5.69054pt]
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{d}&M_{d}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\to\quad\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{d}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}W_{L}^{\dagger}\,m_{d}&W_{L}^{\dagger}\,\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{d}\\[5.69054pt]
{W_{L}^{D}}^{\dagger}\,\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{d}&{W_{L}^{D}}^{\dagger}\,M_{d}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{W}_{R}^{d}\,.

The unitary rotations
\mathcal{W}^{q}_{R}
that appear on the right-hand side of these transformations are useful in shaping the mass matrices
\mathcal{M}_{q}
. Under specific conditions, they can be employed to eliminate some portion of the matrix and produce a triangular matrix.
In particular, it is worth noting that any complex square matrix
\mathcal{M}
can be expressed as the product of a lower-triangular matrix
L
and a unitary matrix
W
to its right,
\mathcal{M}=LW
. Alternatively, it can be written as the product of an upper-triangular matrix
R
and a different unitary matrix
W^{\prime}
to its right, i.e.,
\mathcal{M}=RW^{\prime}
. This follows from the well-known QR decomposition, which states that any complex square matrix
A
can be written as
A=QR
, with
Q
being a unitary matrix and
R
being an upper (or right) triangular matrix. By QR-decomposing
\mathcal{M}^{T}
, one finds the decomposition
\mathcal{M}=LQ^{\prime}
, where
Q^{\prime}
is unitary and
L
is lower (or left) triangular. Similarly, by QR-decomposing
\mathcal{M}^{T}\mathcal{P}
, where
\mathcal{P}
is the anti-diagonal permutation matrix, one can demonstrate that it is possible to write
\mathcal{M}=R^{\prime}Q^{\prime\prime}
, where
Q^{\prime\prime}
is unitary and
R^{\prime}
is upper triangular.

The matrices
\mathcal{W}^{u}_{R}
and
\mathcal{W}^{d}_{R}
can then be used to eliminate the unitary factors
W^{(\prime)}
and render the mass matrices
\mathcal{M}_{u}
and
\mathcal{M}_{d}
lower or upper triangular, as desired. Additionally, they can be utilized to permute the columns of these matrices.
As a result:

•

A WB where
\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}=0
exists for all
n_{q}
(
vanishing
\,\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt
WB).

•

A WB where
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{q}=0
exists for all
n_{q}
(vanishing
\,\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu
WB).

•

A WB where
m_{q}=0
is guaranteed to exist only if
n_{q}\geq n_{f}=3
.

•

A WB where
M_{q}=0
is guaranteed to exist only if
n_{q}\leq n_{f}=3
.

Typically, only one of the sub-matrices
m
,
\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt
,
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu
, or
M
can be made to vanish at a time through a WB transformation. This means that one can impose at most one of the above conditions at a time. However, the vanishing conditions can be enforced separately in the up and down sectors. Therefore, in each sector, it is always possible to choose a weak basis where the mass matrix has the following form:

\mathcal{M}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}m&\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt\\[5.69054pt]
0&M\end{pmatrix}\!\!\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathcal{M}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}m&0\\[5.69054pt]
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu&M\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.37)

Furthermore, in each sector, the matrix blocks located in the lower-right position, denoted as
M
, can always be transformed into a diagonal form, denoted as
D^{\prime}
, with real and non-negative entries using a secondary WB transformation. This transformation involves rotating the left and right unitary matrices in the singular value decomposition of
M
by employing suitable choices of
W^{U,D}_{L}
and
\mathcal{W}^{u,d}_{R}
(which are now block diagonal). This process may impact the matrix
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu
if it is present, which is currently a general complex matrix.

On the other hand, the matrix blocks
m
located in the upper-left position in each sector cannot be simultaneously converted into a diagonal form using the same procedure. This limitation arises due to the use of a single
W_{L}
matrix, which is common to both the up and down sectors.

Consequently, if one begins with upper-triangular mass matrices
\mathcal{M}_{u}
and
\mathcal{M}_{d}
, the most that can be achieved through the WB transformation is a configuration where a unitary rotation remains in one of the sectors (for example, the down sector).

\mathcal{M}_{u}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}d_{u}^{\prime}&\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{u}\\[5.69054pt]
0&D_{u}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad\mathcal{M}_{d}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}V^{\prime}d_{d}^{\prime}&\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{d}\\[5.69054pt]
0&D_{d}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.38)

Here (
minimal vanishing
\,\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu
WB
),
d_{q}^{\prime}
and
D_{q}^{\prime}
are diagonal matrices with real non-negative entries,
V^{\prime}
is the anticipated residual unitary rotation, and
\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}
are general complex matrices.

A similar choice of the WB (Wolfenstein parametrization) has been made in reference
[
147
]
, where the matrix
m_{d}
is allowed to be a general complex matrix.
It is important to note that the primed diagonal matrices, denoted as
d_{q}^{\prime}
and
D_{q}^{\prime}
, differ from the matrices containing the physical masses as defined in eq. (
3.1
).
However, it can be verified that the determinant of
d_{q}^{\prime}
multiplied by the determinant of
D_{q}^{\prime}
is equal to the absolute value of the determinant of
d_{q}
multiplied by the determinant of
D_{q}
, which represents the product of all quark masses of type
q
.
Similarly, it is possible to choose a different WB parametrization where

\mathcal{M}_{u}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}d_{u}^{\prime}&0\\[5.69054pt]
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{u}&D_{u}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad\mathcal{M}_{d}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}V^{\prime}d_{d}^{\prime}&0\\[5.69054pt]
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{d}&D_{d}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

(3.39)

(
minimal vanishing
\,\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt
WB
),
with
d_{q}^{\prime}
and
D_{q}^{\prime}
diagonal,
V^{\prime}
unitary and now
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{q}
general complex.
Finally, by the above procedure, it further follows that one may easily arrange for weak bases like

\mathcal{M}_{u}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}d_{u}^{\prime}&0\\[5.69054pt]
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{u}&D_{u}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad\mathcal{M}_{d}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}V^{\prime}d_{d}^{\prime}&\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{d}\\[5.69054pt]
0&D_{d}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

(3.40)

(
mixed minimal WB
), where both
\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{u}
and
\overline{\!M}\mkern-2.0mu_{d}
are made to vanish.
A closely related WB choice has been made in Ref.
[
148
]
,
where again
m_{d}
has been kept general complex.

By examining eq. (
3.38
), it becomes apparent that for arbitrary values of
n_{u}
and
n_{d}
, it is generally not possible to transform either of the matrices
\mathcal{M}_{u}
or
\mathcal{M}_{d}
into a diagonal form through a WB transformation.

In other words, a diagonal mass matrix in the flavour basis does not typically correspond to a straightforward choice in the WB parametrization.

However, in the presence of vector-like quarks (VLQs), exceptions occur in certain limit cases where either
n_{u}
or
n_{d}
equals zero. In these cases, it is indeed possible to diagonalize one of the mass matrices, either
\mathcal{M}_{u}
or
\mathcal{M}_{d}
, respectively. As an illustration, reference
[
149
]
considers the specific scenario with
n_{u}=0
and
n_{d}=1
, and a WB parametrization is chosen such that
\mathcal{M}_{u}
becomes a diagonal matrix represented by
d_{u}
, while
\mathcal{M}_{d}
takes the form described in eq. (
3.39
).

3.1.1.2

Parameter counting

In the WB choices of eqs. (
3.38
) (
3.39
), (
3.40
), the physical parameter count becomes apparent. Looking at e.g. eq. (
3.38
), one finds

•

n_{f}+n_{q}=3+n_{q}
real parameters (moduli) in the matrices
d_{q}^{\prime}
and
D_{q}^{\prime}
of each sector,

•

n_{f}n_{q}=3n_{q}
complex parameters in each
\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}
matrix, i.e.
3n_{q}
moduli and
3n_{q}
phases in each sector, and

•

n_{f}^{2}=9
parameters in the unitary matrix
V^{\prime}
, corresponding to
\frac{1}{2}n_{f}(n_{f}-1)=3
moduli and
\frac{1}{2}n_{f}(n_{f}+1)=6
phases.

However, it is important to note that, similar to the Standard Model case, there is still some rephasing freedom in the WB parametrization, and not all of the phases mentioned above are physically meaningful.

In order to obtain a WB representation with the minimum number of parameters, one can consider rephasings of the WB where the unitary matrices
W_{L}
,
W_{L}^{U,D}
, and
\mathcal{W}_{R}^{u,d}
are diagonal matrices consisting of phases. By doing so, one can

•

remove
2n_{f}-1=5
phases from
V^{\prime}
by use of the first
n_{f}=3
phases of
\mathcal{W}_{R}^{d}
(commuting with
d_{d}^{\prime}
) and of
W_{L}
, whose effect on
d_{u}^{\prime}
may be cancelled by
\mathcal{W}_{R}^{u}
,

•

remove
n_{q}
phases from
\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}
in each sector via the last
n_{q}
phases of
\mathcal{W}_{R}^{q}
(see also
[
147
]
), whose effects on
D_{q}^{\prime}
may be cancelled by the corresponding
W_{L}^{U,D}
.

Therefore, in this
minimal WB
the mass matrices display a total of
N_{\text{phys}}=10+6(n_{u}+n_{d})
parameters, while keeping the form of eq. (
3.38
). Their breakdown into moduli and phases is summarized in table
3.2
.

Moduli

Phases

Total

\mathcal{M}_{u}
(minimal WB)

3+4n_{u}

2n_{u}

3+6n_{u}

\mathcal{M}_{d}
(minimal WB)

6+4n_{d}

1+2n_{d}

7+6n_{d}

Total

9+4(n_{u}+n_{d})

1+2(n_{u}+n_{d})

10+6(n_{u}+n_{d})

Table 3.2
:
Physical parameter count in the presence of isosinglet VLQs (
n_{f}=3
).

Not only does one recover the SM result when
n_{d}=n_{u}=0
, but one also expects each singlet vector-like quark to bring about 6 new physical parameters: 4 moduli (1 mass and 3 mixing angles) and 2 physical phases.

3.1.2

Deviations from Unitarity, FCNC and Perturbativity

Unlike the neutrino case, very light VLQs are very difficult (if not impossible) to accommodate with current phenomenological data. Hence, deviations from unitary in the quark sector can never be too large.
This statement holds true because, similar to the example of neutrinos - see chapter
2
- there is a seesaw between deviations from unitarity and the new particles’ masses, in order to have sensible Yukawa couplings. This is why one should worry about perturbativity, since for large masses deviations from unitarity are very strongly upper bounded.
The definitions in section
3.1
are useful in theory, but due to the aforementioned fact, more useful formulas can be derived via an approximation valid up to
\mathcal{O}(X^{3})
.

To derive these approximations, it is convenient to simplify eqs. (
3.1
) (
3.29
) and consider a specific WB. The following WB transformation

\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}l}u^{0}_{L}\,\to\,V_{K^{u}_{L}}\,u^{0}_{L}\,,&d^{0}_{L}\,\to\,V_{K^{u}_{L}}\,d^{0}_{L}\,,\\[5.69054pt]
U^{0}_{L}\,\to\,V_{\overline{\!K}\,\!^{u}_{L}}\,U^{0}_{L}\,,&D^{0}_{L}\,\to\,V_{\overline{\!K}\,\!^{d}_{L}}\,D^{0}_{L}\,,\end{array}

(3.41)

removes the matrices
V_{K^{u}_{L}}
,
V_{\overline{\!K}\,\!^{d}_{L}}
and
V_{\overline{\!K}\,\!^{u}_{L}}
from eq. (
3.29
) without modifying the charged currents.
Now, doing an approximation valid for small
X
,

\big(\mathds{1}_{3}+X^{\dagger}X\big)^{-1/2}\simeq\mathds{1}_{3}-\frac{1}{2}X^{\dagger}X\,,\quad\big(\mathds{1}_{n}+XX^{\dagger}\big)^{-1/2}\simeq\mathds{1}_{n}-\frac{1}{2}XX^{\dagger}\,,

(3.42)

valid up to
\mathcal{O}(X^{3})
, which is the same as saying that terms of higher order are negligible.
It is now possible to write eq. (
3.1
) in the following way

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}^{d}_{L}&\simeq\begin{pmatrix}V^{\prime}&0\\
0&\mathds{1}_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathds{1}_{3}-\frac{1}{2}\Theta^{\dagger}_{d}\Theta_{d}&\Theta_{d}^{\dagger}\\
-\Theta_{d}&\mathds{1}_{n_{d}}-\frac{1}{2}\Theta_{d}\Theta^{\dagger}_{d}\end{pmatrix}\,,\\[2.84526pt]
\mathcal{V}^{u}_{L}&\simeq\begin{pmatrix}\mathds{1}_{3}-\frac{1}{2}\Theta^{\dagger}_{u}\Theta_{u}&\Theta_{u}^{\dagger}\\
-\Theta_{u}&\mathds{1}_{n_{u}}-\frac{1}{2}\Theta_{u}\Theta^{\dagger}_{u}\end{pmatrix}\,,\end{split}

(3.43)

where
V^{\prime}=V_{K^{u}_{L}}^{\dagger}V_{K^{d}_{L}}
. Furthermore,
X^{q}_{L}
was replaced by
\Theta_{q}
, to emphasize that an approximation has been employed, this convention will be adopted throughout this thesis.
In this notation, the quark mixing matrix is given by

V\simeq\begin{pmatrix}V^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\Theta^{\dagger}_{u}\Theta_{u}V^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}V^{\prime}\Theta^{\dagger}_{d}\Theta_{d}&V^{\prime}\Theta_{d}^{\dagger}\\
\Theta_{u}V^{\prime}&\Theta_{u}V^{\prime}\Theta_{d}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(3.44)

where the
3\times 3
unitary matrix
V^{\prime}
is related to the CKM matrix
V_{\text{CKM}}
through

V_{\text{CKM}}\simeq\bigg(\mathds{1}_{3}-\frac{1}{2}\Theta^{\dagger}_{u}\Theta_{u}\bigg)\,V^{\prime}\,\bigg(\mathds{1}_{3}-\frac{1}{2}\Theta^{\dagger}_{d}\Theta_{d}\bigg)\,,

(3.45)

an expression valid up to terms of order
\Theta^{3}_{q}
, as expected. Note that the deviation from unitarity is evident for nonzero
\Theta_{u}
or
\Theta_{d}
.

It is now appropriate to discuss the standard quark FCNC and their relationship to the deviations from unitarity of the CKM matrix, taking advantage of the general and exact parameterization introduced before.

As demonstrated in section
3.1
, in models involving singlet VLQs, the FCNC affecting the standard quarks in each sector can be entirely determined by the matrices
F^{d}=V^{\dagger}V
and
F^{u}=VV^{\dagger}
.

The standard quark FCNC are controlled by the upper-left
3\times 3
block of each
F_{q}
matrix, and these are equal to the identity matrix,
\mathds{1}_{3}
, when the CKM matrix
V_{\textrm{CKM}}
, which represents the upper-left
3\times 3
block of the full quark mixing matrix,
V
, is unitary.

Hence, FCNC are closely linked to the aforementioned deviations from unitarity. This connection becomes explicit when considering the precise parameterization given by equation (
3.1
), used to derive eq. (
3.32
). When the deviations from unitarity are small, this relation may be approximated by

F^{q}\simeq\begin{pmatrix}\mathds{1}_{3}-\Theta_{q}^{\dagger}\Theta_{q}&\Theta_{q}^{\dagger}\\
\Theta_{q}&\Theta_{q}\Theta^{\dagger}_{q}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(3.46)

with the replacement
X_{L}^{q}\to\Theta_{q}
, an expression valid up to terms of order
\Theta^{3}_{q}
.
Note that in the limit
\Theta_{q}\to 0
,
F^{q}
approaches the diagonal matrix
\diag(1,1,1,0,\ldots,0)
.

In order to better illustrate the relation between FCNC and deviations from unitarity, the following useful parameters are introduced

\Delta^{d}_{n}\equiv 1-\sum^{3+n_{u}}_{j=1}\big|V_{jn}\big|^{2}\,,\qquad\Delta^{u}_{n}\equiv 1-\sum^{3+n_{d}}_{j=1}\big|V_{nj}\big|^{2}\,.

(3.47)

Here,
\Delta^{d}_{n}
is a test of the unitarity of the
n
-th column of
V
, with
n=1,...,3+n_{d}
, and
\Delta^{u}_{n}
is a test of the unitarity of the
n
-th row of
V
, with
n=1,...,3+n_{u}
. Since
F^{d}=V^{\dagger}V
and
F^{u}=VV^{\dagger}
, they are also given by

\Delta^{q}_{n}=1-F^{q}_{nn}\,.

(3.48)

Combining this equation with eqs. (
3.32
) (
3.46
) gives

\Delta^{q}_{n}=1-\Big(\mathds{1}_{3}+X^{q^{\dagger}}_{L}X^{q}_{L}\Big)^{-1}_{nn}\simeq\big(\Theta_{q}^{\dagger}\Theta_{q}\big)_{nn}=\sum^{n_{q}}_{j=1}\big|(\Theta_{q})_{nj}\big|^{2}\,.

(3.49)

Although these definitions are useful in theory, these parameters are not easily measurable, since one, currently, only has access to the CKM elements of
V
.
Hence, experimentalists test the unitarity of
V_{\text{CKM}}
directly (as presented in the beginning of section
3.2.1
).
Taking this into account, definitions for parameters that test the unitarity of the CKM matrix follow. To probe the unitarity of the columns,

\delta^{d}_{n}\equiv 1-\sum^{3}_{j=1}\big|V_{jn}\big|^{2}=1-|V_{un}|^{2}-|V_{cn}|^{2}-|V_{tn}|^{2}

(3.50)

and that of its rows,

\delta^{u}_{n}\equiv 1-\sum^{3}_{j=1}\big|V_{nj}\big|^{2}=1-|V_{nd}|^{2}-|V_{ns}|^{2}-|V_{nb}|^{2}\,,

(3.51)

with
n=1,2,3
here.
By comparing these expressions with eq. (
3.47
), a connection between the parameters
\Delta^{q}_{n}
and
\delta^{q}_{n}
becomes apparent

\delta^{d}_{n}-\Delta^{d}_{n}=\sum^{n_{u}}_{j=1}\big|V_{j+3,n}\big|^{2}\,,\qquad\delta^{u}_{n}-\Delta^{u}_{n}=\sum^{n_{d}}_{j=1}\big|V_{n,j+3}\big|^{2}\,.

(3.52)

By using eq. (
3.31
) and eq. (
3.43
), these relations become

\begin{split}\delta^{d}_{n}-\Delta^{d}_{n}&=\sum^{n_{u}}_{j=1}\big|(X^{u}_{L}V_{\text{CKM}})_{jn}\big|^{2}\simeq\sum^{n_{u}}_{j=1}\big|(\Theta_{u}V^{\prime})_{jn}\big|^{2}\,,\\
\delta^{u}_{n}-\Delta^{u}_{n}&=\sum^{n_{d}}_{j=1}\big|(V_{\text{CKM}}X^{d\dagger}_{L})_{nj}\big|^{2}\simeq\sum^{n_{d}}_{j=1}\big|(V^{\prime}\Theta^{\dagger}_{d})_{nj}\big|^{2}\,.\end{split}

(3.53)

Finally,
it is instructive to consider
the following limit scenarios:

•

When there are only down-type VLQs in the theory (
n_{u}=0
,
n_{d}\neq 0
), the rows of
V
are orthonormal, which gives
F^{u}=\mathds{1}_{3}
and
\Delta^{u}_{n}=0
.
However, since these rows have more than three entries, the rows of the
3\times 3

V_{\text{CKM}}
generically deviate from unitarity, i.e. they are not orthonormal,
\delta^{u}_{n}=\sum^{n_{d}}_{j=1}|V_{n,j+3}|^{2}\neq 0
.
Meanwhile, since in these models
V
is a
3\times(3+n_{d})
matrix,

\delta^{d}_{n}=\Delta^{d}_{n}
, and the columns of
V_{\text{CKM}}
are also not expected to be orthonormal.

•

When there are only up-type VLQs in the theory (
n_{u}\neq 0
,
n_{d}=0
), the columns of
V
are orthonormal, i.e.
F^{d}=\mathds{1}_{3}
and
\Delta^{d}_{n}=0
.
Nevertheless,
\delta^{d}_{n}=\sum^{n_{u}}_{j=1}|V_{j+3,n}|^{2}\neq 0
generically.
Meanwhile, since in these models
V
is a
(3+n_{u})\times 3
matrix,
\delta^{u}_{n}=\Delta^{u}_{n}
. As in the previous case, neither the rows nor the columns of the
3\times 3

V_{\text{CKM}}
are expected to be orthonormal in general.

In the following sections, section
3.2.1
and section
3.2.2
, useful parameterizations for
V
and
F^{u,d}
in such limit cases, where only one VLQ isosinglet is added to the SM, are discussed.

As stated in section
3.1
, the perturbativity of the Yukawa couplings of SM quarks to the Higgs boson is one of the most important constraints on the mixing matrix,
V
, and the matrices that control its deviations from unitarity
X_{L}^{u}
and
X_{L}^{d}
.
To make the interplay between deviations from unitarity, mass scales and perturbativity explicit, consider the upper-left block on both sides of the relation
\mathcal{M}_{q}\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{q}=\mathcal{V}^{q}_{L}\mathcal{D}^{2}_{q}\mathcal{V}^{q\dagger}_{L}

m_{q}m^{\dagger}_{q}+\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt^{\dagger}_{q}=K^{q}_{L}\big(d^{2}_{q}+X^{q\dagger}_{L}D^{2}_{q}X^{q}_{L}\big)K^{q\dagger}_{L}\,,

(3.54)

combining it with eq. (
3.29
), this relation implies

\displaystyle\textrm{Tr}\big(m_{q}m^{\dagger}_{q}+\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt^{\dagger}_{q}\big)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\Big[\big(d^{2}_{q}+X^{q\dagger}_{L}D^{2}_{q}X^{q}_{L}\big)\big(1+X^{q\dagger}_{L}X^{q}_{L}\big)^{-1}\Big]

(3.55)

\displaystyle\simeq\sum^{3}_{i=1}m^{2}_{q_{i}}+\sum^{3}_{i=1}\sum^{n_{q}}_{j=1}\big|(\Theta_{q})_{ij}\big|^{2}M^{2}_{q_{j}}\,,

where in the second line an approximation valid for small deviations from unitarity was considered. Here,
m_{q_{i}}
and
M_{q_{j}}
refer, respectively, to the the light and heavy quark masses in a given sector.
At this stage, the link to perturbativity is disclosed via the definitions
m_{q}=\frac{v}{\sqrt{2}}Y_{q}
and
\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}=\frac{v}{\sqrt{2}}\overline{Y}\!_{q}

and the requirement that

\textrm{Tr}\big(m_{q}m^{\dagger}_{q}+\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt_{q}\overline{m\hskip-0.86108pt}\hskip 0.86108pt^{\dagger}_{q}\big)<\rho\,m^{2}_{t}\,,

(3.56)

for some
\mathcal{O}(1-10)
numerical factor
\rho
. This requirements reflects the fact that the individual Yukawa couplings should be at most
\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
, or the theory may not be perturbative.
Combining this bound with eq. (
3.55
), one finds

\sum_{i,j}\big|(\Theta_{q})_{ij}\big|^{2}M^{2}_{q_{j}}<\rho\,m^{2}_{t}-\sum_{i}m^{2}_{q_{i}}\,,

(3.57)

showing how the deviations from unitarity are limited by the ratio between the electroweak and VLQ scales in perturbative models.

For illustrative purposes, a model
with only a single up-type VLQ
T
, has

\sum_{i,j}\big|(\Theta_{u})_{ij}\big|^{2}M^{2}_{u_{j}}=\left(|\Theta_{u}|^{2}+|\Theta_{c}|^{2}+|\Theta_{t}|^{2}\right)m^{2}_{T}\penalty\ ,

(3.58)

where the
3\times 1
matrix,
\Theta_{u}
, here is implicitly defined as
(\Theta_{u},\Theta_{c},\Theta_{t})^{T}
.
In such a model, eq. (
3.57
) becomes

\sqrt{|\Theta_{u}|^{2}+|\Theta_{c}|^{2}+|\Theta_{t}|^{2}}\lesssim\frac{m_{t}}{m_{T}}\,,

(3.59)

which shows that all deviations from unitarity must be smaller than
\mathcal{O}(m_{t}/m_{T})
, as expected.

3.1.3

CP Violation in the Presence of VLQs

CP violation arises from a conflict between the CP transformations demanded by the gauge interactions and those required by the Yukawa interactions. The most general CP transformation is defined as the one that preserves the gauge interactions
[
150
]
. Subsequently, one may focus on the restrictions that CP invariance imposes on the Yukawa couplings. In the framework of the SM, it can be readily demonstrated that CP invariance implies
[
151
]

I_{\textrm{SM}}\,\equiv\,\textrm{Tr}\left[Y_{u}Y_{u}^{\dagger},\,Y_{d}Y_{d}^{\dagger}\right]^{3}\,=\,0\,,

(3.60)

where
Y_{u,d}
are the Yukawa couplings, i.e.
I_{\textrm{SM}}=0
is a necessary condition for CP invariance. The quantity
I_{\textrm{SM}}
is a weak-basis invariant, meaning it remains the same in any weak-basis representation. For a single generation, this condition trivially vanishes. For two generations, it can be shown that
I_{\textrm{SM}}=0
follows from the Hermiticity of
Y_{q}Y_{q}^{\dagger}
(
q=u,d
). However, with three generations, equation (
3.60
) imposes non-trivial constraints on the Yukawa couplings. As
I_{\textrm{SM}}
can be evaluated in any weak-basis representation, one may consider the basis where
Y_{u}
is diagonal and real. In this case, the following relationship holds

\displaystyle I_{\textrm{SM}}

\displaystyle=\,6i\,(y_{1}^{2}-y_{2}^{2})(y_{2}^{2}-y_{3}^{2})(y_{3}^{2}-y_{1}^{2})\,\im\left(H_{12}H_{23}H_{31}\right)

(3.61)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\textrm{\tiny EWSB}}\,\frac{6i}{v^{12}}\,(m_{c}^{2}-m_{u}^{2})(m_{t}^{2}-m_{c}^{2})(m_{t}^{2}-m_{u}^{2})

\displaystyle(m_{b}^{2}-m_{s}^{2})(m_{b}^{2}-m_{d}^{2})(m_{s}^{2}-m_{d}^{2})\,\im\left(V_{us}V_{cb}V_{ub}^{*}V_{cs}^{*}\right)\,,

where
Y_{u}=\diag(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
and
H\equiv Y_{d}Y_{d}^{\dagger}
. Moreover, in this case eq. (
3.60
) is both a necessary and sufficient condition to have CP invariance in the SM. It should be noted that in the case of three generations, the condition for CP invariance is equivalent to
\det[M_{u}M_{u}^{\dagger},M_{d}M_{d}^{\dagger}]=0

[
152
,
153
]
, where this determinant is proportional to
I_{\textrm{SM}}
. For more than three generations, equation (
3.60
) remains a necessary condition for CP invariance, but it is no longer sufficient (cf. ref.
[
35
]
).

In the first line of equation (
3.61
), the invariant
I_{\textrm{SM}}
is expressed solely in terms of Yukawa couplings, and the analysis is conducted at the Lagrangian level, prior to Electroweak Symmetry Breaking (EWSB). However, it is also possible to perform this analysis after EWSB, as shown in the second line of equation (
3.61
), where the invariant is written in terms of quark masses and mixing. It is worth emphasizing that the CP conditions can be investigated at the Lagrangian level without necessarily relying on quark masses and mixing. The pioneering result by Kobayashi and Maskawa
[
154
]
could have been derived without much effort by considering a general CP transformation without invoking the matrix
V_{\textrm{CKM}}
.

CP violation is present in the quark sector of the SM if and only if any of the rephasing-invariant functions of the CKM matrix is not real
[
35
]
. Examples of such functions include
quartets
like
Q_{uscb}\equiv V_{us}V_{cb}V_{ub}^{*}V_{cs}^{*}
. In the SM quark sector, there is a single physical phase parameter, as demonstrated in section
3.1.1.2
with
n_{u}=n_{d}=0
, which implies the equality of the imaginary parts of all quartets up to a sign
[
152
,
153
,
155
]
. By defining
I\equiv\im Q_{uscb}
, it becomes evident that the condition
I=0
is not only consistent with CP invariance but also implies it, as evident from eq. (
3.61
). Moreover, the quantity
|I|
has a geometrical interpretation, as it corresponds to twice the area of any unitarity triangle. Taking into consideration the magnitudes of the moduli of
V_{\text{CKM}}
elements, the standard and most useful unitarity triangle is defined by the unitarity relation:

V_{ud}V_{ub}^{*}+V_{cd}V_{cb}^{*}+V_{td}V_{tb}^{*}=0\,,

(3.62)

with each term in the sum corresponding to a side of the triangle in the complex plane.

The experimental determination of the physical CP-violating phases present in the quark mixing matrix serves as a rigorous test of the Standard Model (SM). Without enforcing unitarity, the
3\times 3
matrix
V_{\text{CKM}}
contains four independent rephasing-invariant phases, as five out of the initial nine phases can be eliminated through the rephasing of the standard quark fields, as discussed earlier. It is crucial to emphasize that this result is entirely general and holds true
regardless of the presence of VLQs
and without relying on the existence of extra generations
These 4 phases can taken to be
[
156
,
157
]
:

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\equiv\,\arg\left(-V_{cd}V_{tb}V_{cb}^{*}V_{td}^{*}\right)\,,

(3.63)

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\equiv\,\arg\left(-V_{ud}V_{cb}V_{ub}^{*}V_{cd}^{*}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\chi\text{ or }\beta_{s}

\displaystyle\equiv\,\arg\left(-V_{cb}V_{ts}V_{cs}^{*}V_{tb}^{*}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\chi^{\prime}\text{ or }\beta_{K}

\displaystyle\equiv\,\arg\left(-V_{us}V_{cd}V_{ud}^{*}V_{cs}^{*}\right)\,.

Another commonly used definition is
\alpha\equiv\arg\left(-V_{td}V_{ub}V_{tb}^{*}V_{ud}^{*}\right)
, however, it is not an independent phase since it is related to the phases mentioned earlier by
\alpha+\beta+\gamma=\pi
(mod
2\pi
) by definition.

The matrix
V_{\text{CKM}}
, which describes the mixing among the known quarks, comprises 13 measurable quantities: 9 moduli and 4 rephasing-invariant phases. In the limit of the Standard Model where
V_{\text{CKM}}
is unitary, there are
n_{f}^{2}=9
exact and independent relations among these quantities, such as the one given in equation (
3.62
), resulting in a total of 4 independent parameters. These 4 parameters are typically chosen as the standard 3 mixing angles and 1 CP-violation phase. However, they may also be taken to be i) the phases in eq. (
3.63
), or ii) four independent moduli. In fact, ref.
[
156
]
demonstrated that assuming
3\times 3
unitarity of
V_{\text{CKM}}
allows the rephasing-invariant phases to be used for reconstructing the entire CKM matrix. In the SM limit, the phases
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
also correspond to the inner angles of the standard unitarity triangle mentioned earlier. Alternatively, the
3\times 3
unitary
V_{\text{CKM}}
, up to a two-fold ambiguity and including the strength of CP violation measured by
|I|
, can be reconstructed by taking 4 independent moduli
|V_{ij}|
as input
[
158
,
159
]
. An additional sign is needed for the complete reconstruction since the moduli do not determine the sign of CP violation. Finally, it is important to note that physical processes can only depend on WB invariant quantities. It has been shown
[
159
]
that in the SM, any four independent moduli of
V_{\text{CKM}}
can be computed from the four WB invariants

\textrm{Tr}(H_{u}H_{d})\,,\quad\textrm{Tr}(H_{u}H^{2}_{d})\,,\quad\textrm{Tr}(H^{2}_{u}H_{d})\,,\quad\textrm{Tr}(H^{2}_{u}H^{2}_{d})\,,

(3.64)

and the six known quark masses, with
H_{q}\equiv Y_{q}Y_{q}^{\dagger}
(
q=u,d
).

The presence of VLQs introduces new sources of CP violation, as demonstrated in section
3.1.1.2
. To illustrate this, one may consider the case where a single up-type VLQ is added to the SM (
n_{u}=1
,
n_{d}=0
), while taking the extreme chiral limit
m_{u}=m_{c}=m_{d}=m_{s}=0
. In this chiral limit,
I_{\text{SM}}=0
, meaning there is no CP violation in the SM since the masses of same-charge quarks are degenerate.
However, the introduction of VLQ interactions allows for the presence of CP-violating phases even in this chiral limit
[
160
]
. Specifically, for
n_{u}=1
and
n_{d}=0
, it is possible to choose a WB, see section
3.1.1
, in which
\mathcal{M}_{d}
is diagonal through appropriate choices of
W_{L}
and
\mathcal{W}^{d}_{R}
, while
\mathcal{M}_{u}=\mathcal{V}^{u}_{L},\mathcal{D}_{u}
with
\mathcal{V}_{R}^{u}
being cancelable by a suitable
\mathcal{W}^{u}_{R}
. Consequently, the up-quark mass matrix has two vanishing columns in the chiral limit. By using the remaining rotation and rephasing freedoms, it is possible to arrive at a WB where

\mathcal{M}_{u}\sim\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\times&0\\
0&0&0&\times\\
0&0&\times&\ast\\
0&0&\times&\times\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad\mathcal{M}_{d}\sim\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&m_{b}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.65)

In this WB, all entries in the up-quark mass matrix are real except for the element denoted by “
\ast
”, which is complex and cannot be removed in general. The presence of this complex element implies the non-vanishing of a WB invariant
[
148
,
161
]
that is proportional to both
\im(\ast)
and
m_{b}^{2}
, indicating a violation of CP symmetry beyond the SM. The chiral limit is not only physically natural for studying CP violation beyond the SM but also phenomenologically relevant in practice. In high-energy collisions, such as those at the TeV scale, the masses of light fermions are negligible, and distinguishing light quark jets becomes impractical. Therefore, significant CP violation at high energy is expected to originate from beyond the SM physics.

In the presence of VLQs, the extraction of physical phases from data requires reexamination. The mixing matrix
V
is now of size
(3+n_{u})\times(3+n_{d})
and generally non-unitary. As discussed earlier, its upper-left
3\times 3
block
V_{\text{CKM}}
contains 4 independent physical phases, as shown in eq. (
3.63
). Without loss of generality, one can adopt the following phase convention
[
35
]

V=\hbox to221.61pt{\vbox to83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.66669pt\lower-37.8223pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{}{{}}{{}}{{}}{
{}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
