{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{80.9952pt}{37.05862pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{67}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.

(3.66)

In the presence of NP effects, the extraction of the CKM phases from experimental data can be affected. In models with VLQs, where NP contributions are negligible in weak processes where the SM dominates at tree level, the determination of
\gamma
and the moduli from the first two rows of
V_{\text{CKM}}
remains unaffected. However, the extraction of
\beta
and
\chi
from
B
meson decays may be contaminated by NP effects. Experiments may instead be sensitive to the combinations
\bar{\beta}\equiv\beta-\phi_{d}
and
\bar{\chi}\equiv\chi+\phi_{s}
, where
\phi_{q}
(
q=d,s
) are phases parameterising NP-induced deviations from the SM in the neutral meson systems
B^{0}_{q}
–
\overline{B^{0}_{q}}
. For example, the decay
B_{d}^{0}\to J/\psi,K
is sensitive to
\sin 2(\bar{\beta}-\chi^{\prime})\simeq\sin 2\bar{\beta}
, while the decay
B_{s}^{0}\to J/\psi,\phi
measures
\sin 2\bar{\chi}
.

In the context of the SM or NP scenarios where
V_{\text{CKM}}
is unitary, the remaining phase
\chi^{\prime}
is expected to be extremely small, with a value of
\chi^{\prime}\sim 6\times 10^{-4}
[
162
]
. Even if
V_{\text{CKM}}
is not strictly unitary but can still be described as the upper-left block of an auxiliary
(3+n_{u}+n_{d})\times(3+n_{u}+n_{d})
unitary matrix
[
147
]
, a bound can still be placed on
\chi^{\prime}
[
163
,
35
]
. Considering the CKM moduli within their
3\sigma
ranges
[
164
]
, it has been found that
|\chi^{\prime}|
is constrained to be less than or around
0.06
, which is approximately equivalent to
3^{\circ}
. Consequently, in most cases of interest, this phase can be safely neglected. Similarly, the phase
\chi
can be significantly larger than the SM expectation
\chi\sim 0.02
in models involving up-type VLQs, but not in models solely comprising down-type VLQs
[
162
]
. Up-type VLQs have been considered to address tensions in the data by introducing a non-zero
\phi_{d}
[
165
]
.

Furthermore, both up-type and down-type VLQs have been explored to potentially explain apparent deviations from unitarity in the first row of
V_{\text{CKM}}
[
22
,
23
,
34
]
. It is important to note that in the presence of possible deviations from unitarity, the familiar SM unitary triangle may be modified. For instance, in extensions involving only one VLQ, the unitary triangle may be replaced by various quadrangles
[
160
]
. As a result, instead of having only one CP-odd invariant, proportional to
\im\left(V_{us}V_{cb}V_{ub}^{*}V_{cs}^{*}\right)
as in the SM, one may obtain several rephasing-invariants arising from other quartets. These new invariants are proportional to
\im\left(V_{ij}V_{km}V_{im}^{*}V_{kj}^{*}\right)
and, in general, are not equal but may be partly related to each other.

3.2

Models with
n_{d}=0
or
n_{u}=0

To illustrate some features of VLQ models, this section will discuss models where only up-like (down-like) VLQs are added to the SM, hence
n_{d}=0
(
n_{u}=0
). These models are relevant in light of the recent CKM unitarity problem
[
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
]
, as they are a natural and simple solution for it. Imposing unitarity of the CKM matrix in the first row gives

|V_{ud}|^{2}+|V_{us}|^{2}+|V_{ub}|^{2}=1\,.

(3.67)

However, considering the fact that
|V_{ub}|^{2}
is much smaller than the other two entries, with
|V_{ub}|^{2}\simeq 1.6\times 10^{-5}
, the following approximate relation

|V_{ud}|^{2}+|V_{us}|^{2}\simeq 1\,,

(3.68)

is valid up to
\mathcal{O}\left(|V_{ub}|^{2}\right)
.
Through precise experimental data and improved control over theoretical uncertainties, the values of
|V_{ud}|
,
|V_{us}|
, and
|V_{ud}|/|V_{us}|
have been determined with high accuracy. These results, along with the approximate relation stated in equation (
3.68
), provide stringent tests for the unitarity of the Cabibbo-Kobayashi-Maskawa (CKM) matrix.

At energy scales much lower than the mass of the
W
boson (
M_{W}
), the matrix elements
|V_{ud}|
and
|V_{us}|
play a crucial role in describing the strength of the effective charged-current interaction responsible for the leptonic decays of hadrons involving the valence quarks
u
,
d
, and
s
. Experimental data from neutron decays
[
166
]
primarily contribute to the determination of
|V_{ud}|
, while the entry
|V_{us}|
is mainly obtained from kaon decay data. The ratio
\left|V_{us}/V_{ud}\right|
can be independently determined by comparing the decay rates of specific radiative pion and kaon decays.

However, recent advancements in the determination of form factors and inclusion of radiative corrections for relevant meson and neutron decay processes have resulted in a downward shift in the central value of
|V_{ud}|
. This has led to tension with the constraint imposed by the unitarity of the first row of the CKM matrix
[
24
,
25
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
]
.
Taking into account these new theory calculations with reduced hadronic uncertainties, the authors of Ref.
[
22
]
find:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}|V_{us}|=0.22333(60)\,,&|V_{ud}|=0.97370(14)\,,&\left|V_{us}/V_{ud}\right|=0.23130(50)\,.\end{array}

(3.69)

These results deviate from the unitarity condition in eq. (
3.68
) by
more than
4\sigma
, disfavouring the SM CKM unitarity at this level.
In fact, the values in eq. (
3.69
) are compatible with

|V_{ud}|^{2}+|V_{us}|^{2}=1-\delta\,,

(3.70)

where

\sqrt{\delta}=0.04\pm 0.01\,\quad(95\%\text{ C.L.})\,.

(3.71)

Note that

\delta\equiv\delta_{1}^{u}

(3.72)

was implicitly defined, from the definition of eq. (
3.51
).
Consequently, it is observed that the parameter
\delta
is of the order of
\mathcal{O}(|V_{cb}|^{2})
. Recent lattice calculations performed by the FLAG collaboration
[
167
]
also support these findings. This deviation between the predictions of the Standard Model and experimental data is commonly known as the CKM unitarity problem or Cabibbo anomaly.

To address this discrepancy, new physics beyond the Standard Model is required if it indeed persists. One possible solution involves introducing an additional mixing channel. One approach to achieve this is by introducing a singlet vector-like quark (VLQ) into the framework of the Standard Model. The inclusion of an extra VLQ would result in an enlarged mixing matrix, where the effective CKM mixing is described by a non-unitary
3\times 3
sub-matrix involving the standard quarks. In the literature, models considering both one additional down-type VLQ and one additional up-type VLQ have been explored within this context
[
22
,
23
,
34
,
168
]
.

In theories in which only up-type VLQs are introduced, it is useful to work in a WB where the down quarks mass matrix
\mathcal{M}_{d}
, in this case a
3\times 3
matrix, is diagonal,
d_{d}
. This WB exists when
n_{d}=0
, as stated in section
3.1.1
. In this case, from eq. (
3.10
) one obtains

\mathcal{V}_{L}^{u\dagger}=\begin{pmatrix}V&B_{L}^{u\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(3.73)

where eq. (
3.17
) was used in combination with

A_{L}^{d}=\mathds{1}_{3}\penalty\ ,

(3.74)

which is valid for the WB in use.
\mathcal{V}_{L}^{u\dagger}
is a
(3+n_{u})\times(3+n_{u})
unitary matrix, convenient to parameterise because the parameterisations for unitary matrices are known and the physical
(3+n_{u})\times 3
mixing matrix,
V
, can be read off directly from it -
its first three columns
. Hence, when only one sector has extra mixing channels, this procedure might be more useful and easier to grasp, as one can use parameterisations a la PDG, with extra mixing angles and phases.

Again, for models in which only down-type VLQs are introduced, it is useful to work in a WB where the up quarks mass matrix
\mathcal{M}_{u}
, in this case a
3\times 3
matrix, is diagonal,
d_{u}
. This WB exists when
n_{u}=0
, as stated in section
3.1.1
. In this case, from eq. (
3.10
) one obtains

\mathcal{V}_{L}^{d}=\begin{pmatrix}V\\
B_{L}^{d}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(3.75)

where eq. (
3.17
) was used in combination with

A_{L}^{u\dagger}=\mathds{1}_{3}\penalty\ ,

(3.76)

which is valid for the WB in use.
\mathcal{V}_{L}^{d}
is a
(3+n_{d})\times(3+n_{d})
unitary matrix. Again, the physical
3\times(3+n_{d})
mixing matrix,
V
, can be read off directly from it -
its first three rows
.

3.2.1

Model with
n_{u}=1
,
n_{d}=0

As an example, consider the case
n_{u}=1
, which is the case of ref.
[
34
]
. This reference intended to provide a simple and minimal answer to the CKM unitarity problem.
Consider the following parameterisation for the matrix in eq. (
3.73
) in terms of 6 mixing angles and 3 phases, first used in ref.
[
158
]
,

\mathcal{V}_{L}^{u\dagger}=\mkern-4.0mu\begin{pmatrix}1\\
&1\\
&&c_{34}&s_{34}\\
&&-s_{34}&c_{34}\end{pmatrix}\mkern-8.0mu\begin{pmatrix}1\\
&c_{24}&&s_{24}e^{-i\delta_{24}}\\
&&1\\
&-s_{24}e^{i\delta_{24}}&&c_{24}\end{pmatrix}\mkern-8.0mu\begin{pmatrix}c_{14}&&&s_{14}e^{-i\delta_{14}}\\
&1\\
&&1\\
-s_{14}e^{i\delta_{14}}&&&c_{14}\end{pmatrix}\mkern-8.0mu\begin{pmatrix}&&&0\\
&V_{\text{PDG}}&&0\\
&&&0\\
0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\,,

(3.77)

where
V_{\text{PDG}}
is the standard PDG parameterization of a
3\times 3
unitary matrix
[
169
,
3
]
,

V_{\text{PDG}}=\mkern-4.0mu\begin{pmatrix}c_{12}c_{13}&s_{12}c_{13}&s_{13}e^{-i\delta}\\
-s_{12}c_{23}-c_{12}s_{23}s_{13}e^{i\delta}&c_{12}c_{23}-s_{12}s_{23}s_{13}e^{i\delta}&s_{23}c_{13}\\
s_{12}s_{23}-c_{12}c_{23}s_{13}e^{i\delta}&-c_{12}s_{23}-s_{12}c_{23}s_{13}e^{i\delta}&c_{23}c_{13}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(3.78)

and
c_{ij}=\cos\theta_{ij}
and
s_{ij}=\sin\theta_{ij}
, with
\theta_{ij}\in[0,\pi/2]
,
\delta_{ij}\in[0,2\pi]
. Note that
V_{\text{CKM}}
is described by the traditional PDG picture when all
\theta_{i4}\rightarrow 0
.
With this parameterization, the matrices which control the tree-level FCNC of these models are
F^{d}=\mathds{1}_{3}
and

F^{u}=\mkern-4.0mu\begin{pmatrix}1-s^{2}_{14}&-s_{14}c_{14}s_{24}e^{-i(\delta_{14}-\delta_{24})}&-s_{14}c_{14}c_{24}s_{34}e^{-i\delta_{14}}&-s_{14}c_{14}c_{24}c_{34}e^{-i\delta_{14}}\\
-s_{14}c_{14}s_{24}e^{i(\delta_{14}-\delta_{24})}&1-c^{2}_{14}s^{2}_{24}&-c^{2}_{14}s_{24}c_{24}s_{34}e^{-i\delta_{24}}&-c^{2}_{14}s_{24}c_{24}c_{34}e^{-i\delta_{24}}\\
-s_{14}c_{14}c_{24}s_{34}e^{i\delta_{14}}&-c^{2}_{14}s_{24}c_{24}s_{34}e^{i\delta_{24}}&1-c^{2}_{14}c^{2}_{24}s^{2}_{34}&-c^{2}_{14}c^{2}_{24}s_{34}c_{34}\\
-s_{14}c_{14}c_{24}c_{34}e^{i\delta_{14}}&-c^{2}_{14}s_{24}c_{24}c_{34}e^{i\delta_{24}}&-c^{2}_{14}c^{2}_{24}s_{34}c_{34}&1-c^{2}_{14}c^{2}_{24}c^{2}_{34}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.79)

For small angles and neglecting the
\mathcal{O}(\theta^{3}_{i4})
terms, one can express the latter as

F^{u}\simeq\begin{pmatrix}1-\theta^{2}_{14}&-\theta_{14}\theta_{24}e^{-i(\delta_{14}-\delta_{24})}&-\theta_{14}\theta_{34}e^{-i\delta_{14}}&-\theta_{14}e^{-i\delta_{14}}\\
-\theta_{14}\theta_{24}e^{i(\delta_{14}-\delta_{24})}&1-\theta^{2}_{24}&-\theta_{24}\theta_{34}e^{-i\delta_{24}}&-\theta_{24}e^{-i\delta_{24}}\\
-\theta_{14}\theta_{34}e^{i\delta_{14}}&-\theta_{24}\theta_{34}e^{i\delta_{24}}&1-\theta^{2}_{34}&-\theta_{34}\\
-\theta_{14}e^{i\delta_{14}}&-\theta_{24}e^{i\delta_{24}}&-\theta_{34}&\theta^{2}_{14}+\theta^{2}_{24}+\theta^{2}_{34}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.80)

Comparing eq. (
3.46
) and eq. (
3.80
), one concludes that

\Theta_{u}=-\theta_{14}e^{-i\delta_{14}}\,,\quad\Theta_{c}=-\theta_{24}e^{-i\delta_{24}}\,,\quad\Theta_{t}=-\theta_{34}\,,

(3.81)

where the angles and phases now refer to the parameters introduced in
3.77
.
Like before, the vector
\Theta_{u}
is implicitly defined as
(\Theta_{u},\Theta_{c},\Theta_{t})^{T}
.

Using the definitions in eq. (
3.51
) one can derive for this case

\displaystyle\delta\,\equiv\,\delta_{1}^{u}

\displaystyle=\,1-|V_{ud}|^{2}-|V_{us}|^{2}-|V_{ub}|^{2}\,

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!=\,\left|\mathcal{V}_{L_{41}}^{*}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\,s_{14}^{2}\,,

(3.82)

\displaystyle\delta_{2}^{u}

\displaystyle=\,1-|V_{cd}|^{2}-|V_{cs}|^{2}-|V_{cb}|^{2}\,

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!=\,\left|\mathcal{V}_{L_{42}}^{*}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\,c_{14}^{2}\,s_{24}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\delta_{3}^{u}

\displaystyle=\,1-|V_{td}|^{2}-|V_{ts}|^{2}-|V_{tb}|^{2}\,

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!=\,\left|\mathcal{V}_{L_{43}}^{*}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\,c_{14}^{2}\,c_{24}^{2}\,s_{34}^{2}\,.

hence obtaining

\displaystyle F^{u}\simeq\,\small\begin{pmatrix}1-\delta_{1}^{u}&-\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{u}}\sqrt{\delta_{2}^{u}}e^{-i(\delta_{14}-\delta_{24})}&-\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{u}}\sqrt{\delta_{3}^{u}}e^{-i\delta_{14}}&-\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{u}}e^{-i\delta_{14}}\\
-\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{u}}\sqrt{\delta_{2}^{u}}e^{i(\delta_{14}-\delta_{24})}&1-\delta_{2}^{u}&-\sqrt{\delta_{2}^{u}}\sqrt{\delta_{3}^{u}}e^{-i\delta_{24}}&-\sqrt{\delta_{2}^{u}}e^{-i\delta_{24}}\\
-\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{u}}\sqrt{\delta_{3}^{u}}e^{i\delta_{14}}&-\sqrt{\delta_{2}^{u}}\sqrt{\delta_{3}^{u}}e^{i\delta_{24}}&1-\delta_{3}^{u}&-\sqrt{\delta_{3}^{u}}\\
-\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{u}}e^{i\delta_{14}}&-\sqrt{\delta_{2}^{u}}e^{i\delta_{24}}&-\sqrt{\delta_{3}^{u}}&\delta_{1}^{u}+\delta_{2}^{u}+\delta_{3}^{u}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.83)

Finally, one can conclude that, in approximation,

\delta_{1}^{u}=|\Theta_{u}|^{2}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \delta_{2}^{u}=|\Theta_{c}|^{2}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \delta_{3}^{u}=|\Theta_{t}|^{2}\penalty\ .

(3.84)

This result shows that there is a simple connection between the deviations from unitarity of the rows of the CKM matrix and the standard quark FCNC. This is a consequence of what was discussed in general in section
3.1.2
.

Since in the context of the CKM unitarity problem one has
\delta\equiv\delta_{1}^{u}\gg\delta_{2}^{u}\sim\delta_{3}^{u}
, using eq. (
3.84
) in the perturbativity discussion in section
3.1.2
gives

m_{T}\lesssim\frac{m_{t}}{\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{u}+\delta_{2}^{u}+\delta_{3}^{u}}}\sim\frac{m_{t}}{\sqrt{\delta}}\,.

(3.85)

Which, within the framework of trying to address the CKM unitarity puzzle, see eq. (
3.71
), implies
m_{T}\lesssim 4.4
TeV for
\sqrt{\delta}=0.04
.
Of course that introducing a VLQ does not come without consequences. The rest of this section follows
[
34
]
. The phenomenological impacts of a up-type VLQ will be discussed, and will be used to restrict the parameter space of this model. With that, a global fit will be performed, with the goal of obtaining a region of the parameter space that is allowed by experiment and that can explain the CKM unitarity problem, while predicting a up-type VLQ,
T
, with a mass that can be probed in the current and next round of experiments.

3.2.1.1

New Physics and Global Fit

A variety of measurable quantities can undergo modifications either at tree level or through loop processes when up-type vector-like quarks (VLQs) are present
[
170
,
171
,
172
,
173
]
.
The effects of new physics contributions on neutral meson mixing, specifically in the
D^{0}
,
K^{0}
, and
B^{0}_{d,s}
neutral-meson systems, as well as the enhanced rare decay of the top quark,
t\to qZ
, are particularly significant. These phenomenological quantities play a crucial role in constraining the parameter space of this model. Furthermore, the existence of a new heavy quark,
T
, could potentially allow its production at current colliders if its mass is sufficiently low.

This quark can be generated either through single production or pair production processes
[
174
,
175
,
176
,
177
]
. The dominant contributions to pair production cross sections are dependent solely on the mass of
T
,
m_{T}
. On the other hand, single production mechanisms are controlled by functions related to the elements of the mixing matrix, namely
V_{Td}
,
V_{Ts}
, and
V_{Tb}
, hence more model-dependent.

In scenarios where significant couplings to light quark generations are present, single production processes can dominate pair production by several orders of magnitude within the few TeV energy range. This is primarily due to a larger available phase space, an enhancement from longitudinal gauge bosons, and the involvement of the proton’s valence quarks (
u
and
d
) in the initial state
[
178
,
179
]
.
Our current scenario aligns with this description, as deviations from unitarity in the first row of the CKM matrix necessitate relatively large values for the quantities
|V_{Td}|\sim|F^{u}_{41}|\sim 0.04
(note the approximation
|\mathcal{V}_{L{41}}^{u}|\sim|\mathcal{V}_{L{14}}^{u}|
under the small mixing angle assumption). These quantities govern the primary contributions to the single VLQ production cross-sections via
W
and
Z
boson exchanges.

Regarding the decay of the heavy quark, the decay widths can be determined by generalizing the expressions provided in Appendix B of ref.
[
177
]
(see e.g.
[
180
,
33
]
) and they are also influenced by
|V_{Td}|
and
|F^{u}_{41}|
. In the parameter space of this model, as will be discussed later, decays to the lightest generation can be approximately an order of magnitude more probable compared to decays to the second and third generations.

At the time of writing of ref.
[
34
]
, the most stringent lower limits on the VLQ mass were
m_{T}>1.3
TeV at 95% confidence level (CL) by the ATLAS collaboration
[
181
]
, and
m_{T}\gtrsim 1.0
TeV at 95% CL by the CMS collaboration
[
182
]
, based on searches for pair-produced
T
-quarks.

For more details regarding new physics, effects on physical observables, the best experimental constraints and the most stringent bounds for models with VLQs see section
4.2
.

In order to explore the viability of this model, and to check if it can indeed solve the CKM unitarity process, a numerical scan of the parameter space of the model was performed. Initially, the model is restricted by the magnitudes of the elements in the CKM matrix. The current best-fit values of these elements, without enforcing unitarity, are obtained from
[
3
]
.

|V_{\text{CKM}}|=\begin{pmatrix}0.97370\pm 0.00014&0.2245\pm 0.0008&(3.82\pm 0.24)\times 10^{-3}\\
0.221\pm 0.004&0.987\pm 0.011&(41.0\pm 1.4)\times 10^{-3}\\
(8.0\pm 0.3)\times 10^{-3}&(38.8\pm 1.1)\times 10^{-3}&1.013\pm 0.030\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.86)

In addition, it is assumed that the presence of an up-type VLQ does not affect the value of the phase
\gamma=\arg(-V_{ud}V_{cb}V_{ub}^{*}V_{cd}^{*})

[
183
,
165
]
, which is obtained from SM tree-level dominated
B
decays,
\gamma=(72.1^{+4.1}_{-4.5})^{\circ}\,

[
3
]
.
To assess the goodness of fit, the measure
N\sigma=\sqrt{\chi^{2}}
is employed, where
\chi^{2}
is approximated as the sum of priors,

\chi^{2}=\sum_{ij}\left(\frac{V_{ij}-V_{ij}^{c}}{\sigma(V_{ij})}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\gamma-\gamma^{c}}{\sigma(\gamma)}\right)^{2}\,,

(3.87)

with the superscript
c
denoting central values and
\sigma(\gamma)=4.5^{\circ}\,
. Furthermore, it is assumed
m_{c}(M_{Z})=0.619\pm 0.084
GeV,
m_{t}(M_{Z})=171.7\pm 3.0
GeV
[
184
]
and require
m_{T}>1
TeV, in line with collider bounds.

The values of the new angles and phases compatible with the above criteria are shown as the dashed regions in the correlation plot of Figure
. These regions contract to the solid green
2\sigma
and
3\sigma
contours after all the phenomenological constraints described before are taken into account. These constraints comprise the bounds on
\Delta m_{N}
(
N=D^{0},K^{0},B^{0},B^{0}_{s}
), and
|\epsilon_{K}|
discussed in section
4.2.2
and the perturbativity bound of eq. (
3.85
).
The data suggests that relatively large values for both
\theta_{14}\simeq\sqrt{\delta}
and
\theta_{34}
are favored, indicating a disfavoring of
\theta_{34}=0
at a significance exceeding
2\sigma
. On the other hand,
\theta_{24}
is consistent with zero, and the inclination towards smaller values of this angle is influenced by the constraint imposed by
D^{0}
-
\overline{D}^{0}
mixing (refer to section
4.2.2.2
).

The constraint of perturbativity also limits the permissible values of
m_{T}
, which are depicted in Figure
3.1
against the deviations from unitarity in the first row. As shown by eq. (
3.85
), the maximum value of
m_{T}
relies on the magnitude of these deviations. Assuming
\sqrt{\delta}=0.04
, it follows that
m_{T}\lesssim 5
TeV. Considering the entire
3\sigma
region of the fit, this bound extends to
m_{T}\lesssim 7
TeV.

Figure 3.1
:

Masses
m_{T}
of the new heavy quark and first-row deviations from unitarity (
\sqrt{\delta}\simeq\theta_{14}
) compatible with the bounds on
|V_{ij}|
,
\gamma
, meson mixing,
|\epsilon_{K}|
and with the requirement of perturbativity. The latter constraint imposes an upper limit on
m_{T}
, while lower bounds are set by ATLAS
[
181
]
and CMS
[
182
]
(95% CL).

Finally, in Figure
3.2
and Figure
3.3
the predicted values for the branching ratios of rare top decays
t\to uZ
and
t\to cZ
, respectively, as a function of
\sqrt{\delta}
. These may considerably exceed the SM predictions. One finds
2.0\times 10^{-8}<\text{Br}(t\to uZ)<3.5\times 10^{-7}
at the
2\sigma
level, which is still 3 orders of magnitude below present bounds (see section
4.2.2.1
). The decay
t\to cZ
may instead be arbitrarily suppressed, even at the
2\sigma
level, by the allowed smallness of
\theta_{24}
.

Figure 3.2
:
Branching ratios for the rare top decays
t\rightarrow uZ
as a function of first-row deviations from unitarity
\delta
(
\sqrt{\delta}\simeq\theta_{14}
). These may considerably exceed the SM expectations Br
(t\to uZ)_{\text{SM}}\sim 10^{-16}

[
185
]
(not shown). Dashed contours refer to different values for
\theta_{34}
, in the small angle approximation.

Figure 3.3
:
Branching ratios for the rare top decays
t\rightarrow cZ
as a function of first-row deviations from unitarity
\delta
(
\sqrt{\delta}\simeq\theta_{14}
). These may considerably exceed the SM expectations Br
(t\to cZ)_{\text{SM}}\sim 10^{-14}

[
185
]
(not shown). Dashed contours refer to different values for the product
\theta_{24}\,\theta_{34}
, in the small angle approximation.

Considering the correlation plot in Figure
, the
2\sigma
region of the fit, it is observed that
|V_{41}|>|V_{42}|,|V_{43}|
, indicating that the up-type vector-like quark
T
exhibits stronger coupling to the up quark compared to the charm and top quarks. This challenges the conventional assumption that the new quark primarily couples to the third generation of Standard Model quarks. Consequently, the VLQ is more inclined to decay into quarks of the first generation, as there are no suppressions associated with the masses of these quarks. The decay widths primarily depend on
m_{T}
and
|V_{4j}|^{2}
or
|F_{4j}^{u}|^{2}

[
177
,
33
]
.
A discussion of a low-
\chi^{2}
benchmark belonging to this
2\sigma
region now follows.
The selected point in parameter space corresponds to a mass
m_{T}=1.5
TeV and is described by

\displaystyle\theta_{12}=0.2265\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \theta_{13}=0.003818\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \theta_{23}=0.03998\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\

(3.88)

\displaystyle\theta_{14}\simeq\sqrt{\Delta_{(1)}}=0.03951\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \theta_{24}\simeq\sqrt{\Delta_{2}}=0.002078\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \theta_{34}\simeq\sqrt{\Delta_{3}}=0.01271\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\delta_{13}=0.396\,\pi\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{14}=1.818\,\pi\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{24}=0.728\,\pi\,,

corresponding to
\chi^{2}\simeq 3.2
,
m_{T}\approx\frac{0.36}{\sqrt{\delta}}m_{t}
and to the following values for observables:

\begin{split}&x_{D}\simeq 0.18\%\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \Delta m_{K}\simeq 6.3\times 10^{-13}\penalty\ \text{MeV}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \Delta m_{B}\simeq 3.0\times 10^{-10}\penalty\ \text{MeV}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \\[2.84526pt]
&\Delta m_{B_{s}}\simeq 2.7\times 10^{-10}\penalty\ \text{MeV}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ |\epsilon_{K}|\simeq 1.9\times 10^{-4}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \\[2.84526pt]
&\text{Br}(t\to uZ)\simeq 1.2\times 10^{-7}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{Br}(t\to cZ)\simeq 3.2\times 10^{-10}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \\[2.84526pt]
&\alpha\simeq 84.92^{\circ}\,\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \beta\simeq 23.85^{\circ}\,\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \gamma\simeq 71.23^{\circ}\,\,,\end{split}

(3.89)

where
\alpha=\arg(-V_{td}V_{ub}V_{tb}^{*}V_{ud}^{*})
and
\beta=\arg(-V_{cd}V_{tb}V_{cb}^{*}V_{td}^{*})
. The absolute values of the diagonalizing matrix in eq. (
3.77
) read

|\mathcal{V}_{L}^{\dagger}|\simeq\hbox to206.51pt{\vbox to59.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.66669pt\lower-29.75389pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{}}{{}}{{}}{

(3.90)

where
|V|
is given by the first 3 columns of
|\mathcal{V}_{L}^{\dagger}|
(dashed block) and
|V_{\text{CKM}}|
is the
3\times 3
upper-left block of
|\mathcal{V}_{L}^{\dagger}|
(unbroken line).
The absolute values of the entries of
F^{u}
are

|F^{u}|=\begin{pmatrix}0.99843969&0.00008203&0.00050179&0.03946672\\
0.00008203&0.99999569&0.00002638&0.00207496\\
0.00050179&0.00002638&0.99983863&0.01269224\\
0.03946672&0.00207496&0.01269224&0.001726\end{pmatrix}\,,

(3.91)

As mentioned in section
3.1.2
, this matrix encodes the structure of FCNC and deviations from unitarity of
V_{CKM}
.

Lastly, the mass matrix
\mathcal{M}_{u}
for this particular scenario can be explicitly expressed in the weak basis, where the down-type quark mass matrix is diagonal, denoted as
\mathcal{M}_{d}=\mathcal{D}_{d}=\text{diag}(m_{d},m_{s},m_{b})
. In this basis, the upper-left
2\times 2
block of
\mathcal{M}_{u}
undergoes a rotation resulting in a zero matrix,

|\mathcal{M}_{u}|=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&0&1.39&58.57\\
0&0&6.776&9.775\\
0.001658&15.38&170.9&18.92\\
0.03206&1.486&2.166&1499\\
\end{array}\right)\penalty\ \text{GeV}\,.

(3.92)

The latter rotation can be achieved solely via transformations from the right.
In this WB, one can directly identify
(\mathcal{M}_{u})_{33}\simeq m_{t}
,
(\mathcal{M}_{u})_{44}\simeq m_{T}
, and
{(\mathcal{M}_{u})_{14}}/{(\mathcal{M}_{u})_{44}}\simeq\sqrt{\Delta}
. It is evident in this specific instance that
(\mathcal{M}_{u})_{14}<(\mathcal{M}_{u})_{33}
.

It is worth noting that if in the scenario where a down-type VLQ is added to the SM, the down-type quark mass matrix
\mathcal{M}_{d}
would exhibit similar qualitative characteristics to the previously described
\mathcal{M}_{u}
.
Specifically, in the WB where the up-type quark mass matrix
\mathcal{M}_{u}
is diagonal and the upper-left
2\times 2
block of the down-type quark mass matrix
\mathcal{M}_{d}
is transformed into a zero matrix, the structure of
\mathcal{M}_{d}
would need to resemble the form presented in eq. (
3.92
) to achieve the necessary deviation from unitarity in the first row of the CKM matrix.
In such a basis, one expects
(\mathcal{M}_{d})_{33}\sim m_{b}
, with
m_{b}(M_{Z})\simeq 2.89
GeV
[
186
]
. On the other hand, experimental bounds on the mass
m_{B}
of the new heavy quark in this scenario are close to those on
m_{T}
, i.e.
m_{B}\gtrsim 1
TeV
[
181
,
182
]
, implying a large
(\mathcal{M}_{d})_{44}\sim m_{B}
. The requirement of reproducing the observed deviation from unitarity
\sqrt{\delta}\simeq 0.04
would force
(\mathcal{M}_{d})_{14}\gtrsim 50\penalty\ \text{GeV}\gg(\mathcal{M}_{d})_{33}
. Since the terms in the first three rows of the mass matrix share a common origin in electroweak symmetry breaking, this hierarchy between
(\mathcal{M}_{d})_{14}
and
(\mathcal{M}_{d})_{33}
may not be appealing or plausible in a theory of flavour addressing the gap
m_{b}\ll m_{t}
in third-generation quark masses.

In conclusion, the addition of one up-type VLQ may explain the CKM unitarity problem, in, possibly, a more appealing way - in terms of flavour -, than adding one down-type VLQ.

3.2.2

Model
n_{d}=1
,
n_{u}=0

For completeness and comparison to the case of section
3.2.1
, consider the case where
n_{d}=1
, which is briefly discussed in ref.
[
33
]
. Consider the following parameterisation for the matrix in eq. (
3.75
) in terms of 6 mixing angles and 3 phases

\mathcal{V}_{L}^{d}=\mkern-4.0mu\begin{pmatrix}&&&0\\
&V_{\text{PDG}}&&0\\
&&&0\\
0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\mkern-8.0mu\begin{pmatrix}c_{14}&&&-s_{14}e^{-i\delta_{14}}\\
&1\\
&&1\\
s_{14}e^{i\delta_{14}}&&&c_{14}\end{pmatrix}\mkern-8.0mu\begin{pmatrix}1\\
&c_{24}&&-s_{24}e^{-i\delta_{24}}\\
&&1\\
&s_{24}e^{i\delta_{24}}&&c_{24}\end{pmatrix}\mkern-8.0mu\begin{pmatrix}1\\
&1\\
&&c_{34}&-s_{34}\\
&&s_{34}&c_{34}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.93)

With this choice, the traditional picture of
V_{\text{CKM}}
is recovered when all
\theta_{i4}\to 0
.
Meanwhile, in these models the matrices which control all tree-level FCNC are given by

F^{d}=\mkern-4.0mu\begin{pmatrix}1-s^{2}_{14}&-s_{14}c_{14}s_{24}e^{-i(\delta_{14}-\delta_{24})}&-s_{14}c_{14}c_{24}s_{34}e^{-i\delta_{14}}&-s_{14}c_{14}c_{24}c_{34}e^{-i\delta_{14}}\\
-s_{14}c_{14}s_{24}e^{i(\delta_{14}-\delta_{24})}&1-c^{2}_{14}s^{2}_{24}&-c^{2}_{14}s_{24}c_{24}s_{34}e^{-i\delta_{24}}&-c^{2}_{14}s_{24}c_{24}c_{34}e^{-i\delta_{24}}\\
-s_{14}c_{14}c_{24}s_{34}e^{i\delta_{14}}&-c^{2}_{14}s_{24}c_{24}s_{34}e^{i\delta_{24}}&1-c^{2}_{14}c^{2}_{24}s^{2}_{34}&-c^{2}_{14}c^{2}_{24}s_{34}c_{34}\\
-s_{14}c_{14}c_{24}c_{34}e^{i\delta_{14}}&-c^{2}_{14}s_{24}c_{24}c_{34}e^{i\delta_{24}}&-c^{2}_{14}c^{2}_{24}s_{34}c_{34}&1-c^{2}_{14}c^{2}_{24}c^{2}_{34}\end{pmatrix}

(3.94)

and
F^{u}=\mathds{1}_{3}
.
For small angles,
F^{d}
becomes

F^{d}\simeq\mkern-4.0mu\begin{pmatrix}1-\theta^{2}_{14}&-\theta_{14}\theta_{24}e^{-i(\delta_{14}-\delta_{24})}&-\theta_{14}\theta_{34}e^{-i\delta_{14}}&-\theta_{14}e^{-i\delta_{14}}\\
-\theta_{14}\theta_{24}e^{i(\delta_{14}-\delta_{24})}&1-\theta^{2}_{24}&-\theta_{24}\theta_{34}e^{-i\delta_{24}}&-\theta_{24}e^{-i\delta_{24}}\\
-\theta_{14}\theta_{34}e^{i\delta_{14}}&-\theta_{24}\theta_{34}e^{i\delta_{24}}&1-\theta^{2}_{34}&-\theta_{34}\\
-\theta_{14}e^{i\delta_{14}}&-\theta_{24}e^{i\delta_{24}}&-\theta_{34}&\theta^{2}_{14}+\theta^{2}_{24}+\theta^{2}_{34}\end{pmatrix}\mkern-4.0mu

(3.95)

plus terms of order
\mathcal{O}(\theta^{3}_{i4})
.
Notice that, due to similar choices, eq. (
3.94
) and eq. (
3.79
) are identical. However, this is just a mere coincidence, and fits should prefer different values of the
\theta_{i4}
in each case, since they represent different things.

Again, comparing eq. (
3.46
) and eq. (
3.95
), one concludes that

\Theta_{d}=-\theta_{14}e^{-i\delta_{14}}\,,\quad\Theta_{s}=-\theta_{24}e^{-i\delta_{24}}\,,\quad\Theta_{b}=-\theta_{34}\,,

(3.96)

where the angles and phases now refer to the parameters introduced in
3.93
.
Like before, the vector
\Theta_{d}
is implicitly defined as
(\Theta_{d},\Theta_{s},\Theta_{b})^{T}
.
Fore more details regarding this model and how it can solve the CKM unitarity problem, see section 4 of ref.
[
23
]
.

Chapter 4

New Physics and Experimental Signals

The smoking gun signals that can reveal the presence of ’heavy’
1
1

1

Here ’heavy’ should be understood as having a larger mass than the active ’light’ neutrinos which due to the PLANCK bound
[
110
]
are expected to have a sub eV mass - cf. section
2.3.2
of this thesis.
neutrinos and VLQs primarily hinge on two crucial quantities: their mass and their mixing with their respective SM counterparts.
Notably, the mass of the hypothetical particle is a critical factor, as it determines whether the particle can be produced in experimental setups. In models featuring ’heavy’ neutrinos and VLQs, a certain seesaw between their mass and mixing is expected in order to have electroweak perturbativity. This connection is elaborated upon in the final section of
2.2
and section
2.3.2
for neutrinos, and section
3.1.2
for VLQs. Consequently, these models share a common characteristic: as the mass of the hypothetical particle increases, its mixing with the active counterparts must decrease. This leads to the existence of a fruitful region in the parameter space of these models, where the particle’s mass is not excessively large and its mixing is sufficiently significant to produce measurable effects in observable quantities.

The phenomenological analysis presented in refs.
[
33
,
34
,
31
,
30
]
, which form the basis of this thesis, precisely focuses on this particular region in the parameter space, yielding results that can be probed in ongoing or forthcoming experimental endeavors.

In this chapter, these signals will be reviewed, starting in the neutrino sector and finishing in the quark sector. A comprehensive analysis of each signal will be done, along with an assessment of the existing experimental bounds associated with them. Future prospects and current bounds will be discussed according to the heavy particle’s mass.
For neutrinos, the effects of ’heavy’ neutrinos with a mass of some GeV up to a few TeV and with a mass between the MeV and the eV scale will be analysed, as these were discussed in section
2.1
and in ref.
[
30
]
and in section
2.2
and in ref.
[
31
]
. Finally, for VLQs, only those with a mass of a few TeV - discussed in section
3.2
and in refs.
[
34
,
33
]
, will be considered.

4.1

Neutrinos

As implied earlier in this thesis, in terms of experimental viability, only scenarios with
M\lesssim
TeV hold promise for being tested in the foreseeable future
[
89
]
, as neutrinos within this mass range can potentially leave discernible signatures in ongoing experiments at the energy, cosmic, and intensity frontiers. The next paragraph will list the main signals ’heavy’ Majorana neutrinos may leave in current and future experiments. The rest of the section will contain a brief description of each one and their current experimental bounds.

Neutrinos, if Majorana particles, can in principle be tested via Lepton number violating processes such as the neutrinoless double beta decay
[
187
,
188
,
189
]
.
If their mixing with the active neutrinos is sizeable, they can be produced with enough statistics to declare a discovery in the LHC
[
190
]
. This mixing with their active counterparts can be inferred from the non-unitarity of
3\times 3
leptonic mixing matrix
[
54
,
191
,
56
,
57
,
192
]
and have impact on low-energy observables related to lepton flavour violation (LFV), lepton flavour non-universality (LFNU) and electroweak precision tests
[
52
,
56
]
. Special emphasis will be given to the LFV process
\mu\rightarrow e\gamma

[
95
]
, as it was one of the most restrictive to the parameter space of the models studied in ref.
[
31
]
.
Sizeable deviations from
3\times 3
unitary may also leave a clear trace in short baseline and long baseline oscillation experiments
[
192
,
52
,
78
]
.
Beta decay experiments, such as KATRIN, may also play an important role since the existence of a eV almost-sterile ’heavy neutrino’
[
111
]
or a keV one
[
112
]
changes the shape of the beta decay spectrum. Neutrinos in the discussed mass and mixing ranges may also leave traces of CP Violation at low-energy
[
55
,
31
]
detectable in future oscillation experiments and, if long-lived, leave displaced vertex
[
125
,
127
,
128
]
and resolvable oscillation
[
136
,
131
]
signals at colliders.

To end section
4.1
, a discussion on the prospects of future experiments regarding neutrino physics is presented.

4.1.1

Neutrinoless double beta decay

Neutrinoless double
\beta
decay (
0\nu\beta\beta
) is a a Lepton number violating process
[
187
,
122
]
that consists of

(A,Z)\rightarrow(A,Z+2)+e^{-}+e^{-}\penalty\ ,

(4.1)

where
Z
is the atomic number of a given atom, or, put simply, its number of protons, and
A
is its atomic mass number, or its number of protons and neutrons,
e^{-}
represents an electron.
The decay rate for this process is given by:

\Gamma_{0\nu\beta\beta}=G(Q,Z)|M^{0}_{nuc}|^{2}|=G(Q,Z)|M_{nuc}|^{2}\cdot|m_{\beta\beta}|^{2}\penalty\ ,

(4.2)

where
G(Q,Z)
is a phase-space factor that depends on the nucleus and
M^{0}_{nuc}
is the total nuclear matrix element.
M^{0}_{nuc}
contains the leptonic amplitude
(W^{-}_{\mu})^{*}(p_{3})+(W^{-}_{\nu})^{*}(p_{4})\rightarrow e^{-}(p_{1})+e^{-}(p_{2})
, which has two diagrams (channel t and u) because of the anti-symmetrization of the final state:

M^{0}_{nuc}\propto(M_{t}-M_{u})\penalty\ .

(4.3)

It is only possible to write the amplitude for this process using a lepton number violating vertex, thus making the process only possible if neutrinos are Majorana
[
188
]
. For illustrative purposes, consider the t-channel amplitude, specifically the part that is sandwiched between the spinors,

iM_{t}=\sum_{j}\left[\overline{u(p_{1})}\left(\frac{-ig}{\sqrt{2}}\gamma_{\mu}P_{L}(K)_{1j}\right)\frac{i(\gamma^{\delta}p_{\delta}+m_{j})}{p^{2}-m_{j}^{2}}\left(-\frac{-ig}{\sqrt{2}}\gamma_{\nu}P_{L}(K)_{1j}\right)v(p_{2})\right]\penalty\ ,

(4.4)

where
p=p_{1}-p_{3}=p_{4}-p_{2}
and
e^{-}(p_{2})=\left(e^{+}(p_{2})\right)^{c}
represents the Conjugated Dirac fermion, the vertex connecting momenta
p_{2}
and
p_{4}
exhibits lepton number violation.

In the computation of
|M^{0}_{nuc}|^{2}
, upon evaluating the traces of the leptonic part, it becomes evident that terms proportional to
\gamma^{\delta}p_{\delta}
vanish due to the presence of an odd number of gamma matrices. Consequently, the only surviving terms are those proportional to
m_{j}
. Consequently, by neglecting the neutrino mass in the denominator, it becomes possible to factor out a meaningful quantity,

|M^{0}_{nuc}|^{2}=|M_{nuc}|^{2}\cdot|\sum_{j}(K_{1j})^{2}m_{j}|^{2}\penalty\ ,

(4.5)

where
K
is the
3\times 3
non-unitary leptonic mixing matrix, defined in eq. (
2.19
). Combining eq. (
4.5
) with eq. (
4.2
) yields the definition

m_{\beta\beta}=\sum_{j}(K_{1j})^{2}m_{j}\penalty\ ,

(4.6)

Note that, in general, this quantity is sensitive to all the CP Violating Phases in the leptonic sector of the
n_{R}\nu
SM. The unitary phases, the Dirac phase and the Majorana phases, and the hermitian phases, the phases of the deviations from unitarity, as described in section
2.3.1
.
The quantity
m_{\beta\beta}
changes if
p
of the
n_{R}
"heavy" states have a mass of the order of the light neutrinos,
\sim eV,keV
, such that it is kinematically allowed, since this is a nuclear process
[
105
,
72
,
193
]
. In that case,

m_{\beta\beta}=\sum_{i}^{3}(K_{1i})^{2}m_{i}+\sum_{j}^{p}((KX^{\dagger})_{1j})^{2}M_{j}\penalty\ .

(4.7)

The current experimental bounds on
m_{\beta\beta}
, considering 3 light/active neutrinos and very heavy almost-sterile neutrinos, are depicted graphically in fig.
4.1
:

Figure 4.1
:
m_{\beta\beta}
as given in eq. (
4.6
) as a function of the lightest neutrino mass. The dark shaded regions are
predictions based on best-fit values of neutrino oscillation parameters for the normal ordering (NO) and the inverted ordering (IO), and the light shaded regions indicate the
3\sigma
ranges calculated from oscillation parameter uncertainties
[
194
,
195
]
.
The regions below the horizontal lines are allowed at 90%
C.L. with
\textrm{Xe}^{136}
from KamLAND-Zen
[
196
]
considering an improved phase space factor calculation
[
197
,
198
]
and
commonly used nuclear matrix element estimates: energy density functional (EDF) theory
[
199
,
200
,
201
]
(solid lines), interacting boson model (IBM)
[
202
,
203
]
(dashed lines), shell model
(SM)
[
204
,
205
,
206
]
(dot-dashed lines), and quasi-particle random phase approximation (QRPA)
[
207
,
208
,
209
,
210
,
211
]
(dotted lines). The side
panel shows the corresponding limits for
\textrm{Xe}^{136}
,
\textrm{Ge}^{76}

[
212
]
, and

\textrm{Te}^{130}

[
213
]
, and theoretical model predictions on
m_{\beta\beta}
, (a)
Ref.
[
214
]
, (b) Ref.
[
215
]
, and (c) Ref.
[
216
]
(shaded boxes), in the IO region. Image taken from ref.
[
196
]
.

4.1.1.1

M\gtrsim
MeV

Due to the inverted-ordering (IO) hypothesis of light neutrino masses being disfavoured in global fits of leptonic mixing angles
[
58
,
103
,
59
]
, the prospects of measuring
m_{\beta\beta}
have been affected, since for the normal-ordering (NO) scenario
m_{\beta\beta}
can be significantly smaller. If the ’heavy’ neutrinos all have masses much beyond
\sim 100
MeV, the nuclear scale of the process, then they can’t be produced and contribute to
m_{\beta\beta}
. In that case, the leading experiments in neutrinoless double beta decay, namely KamLAND-Zen
[
196
]
, GERDA
[
212
]
, and CUORE
[
213
]
, currently lack immediate prospects of observing significant signals, especially with IO being effectively excluded. Figure
4.1
presents the current most stringent constraints on
m_{\beta\beta}
under this framework.

4.1.1.2

M\lesssim
MeV
\sim
eV, keV

The layout obviously changes if there are ’heavy’ neutrinos with masses closer to the MeV scale
[
217
]
, since in this case
m_{\beta\beta}
has new contributions and its value might be substantially larger
[
105
,
72
,
193
]
.
For instance, the upper bound of the values of
m_{\beta\beta}
, calculated with eq. (
4.7
), for the cases of section
2.2
ranged from
4.6
meV to
27
meV, below the current experimental upper bounds. Nonetheless, if heavy neutrinos with these masses and mixings exist, a discovery may be imminent in the next round of experiments
[
189
]
.

4.1.2

Prompt Decay Searches at LHC: ATLAS, CMS and LHCb

The LHC is the most powerful proton-proton collider built by mankind and is host to nine approved experiments. The primary experiments involved in the pursuit of new physics are ATLAS, CMS and LHCb. To date, these collisions have taken place at center-of-mass energies of 7, 8, and 13 TeV.

Given the limitations of the LHC, ATLAS and CMS searches mainly focused on heavy neutrinos with masses in the GeV/TeV scale
[
217
]
, as these had the better discovery prospects.
The LHC’s array of detector experiments offers comprehensive coverage across a wide range of potential scenarios related to heavy neutrinos, with multiple searches
[
89
,
218
,
219
,
220
,
221
,
222
]
addressing the matter.

Another experimental configuration within the LHC is the LHCb experiment, which primarily focuses on searching for new physics through the decay of heavy-flavour hadrons. Its unique setup has yielded intriguing indications of potential LFNU, as reported by the LHCb experiment
[
223
,
224
]
. However, these findings require confirmation and establishment with greater statistical significance and preferably through independent measurements.

To summarize, ATLAS, CMS, and LHCb experiments have conducted searches for heavy neutrinos in different regions of mass, active-sterile neutrino mixing, and lifetimes, encompassing both minimal and non-minimal scenarios. This thesis will only cover the minimal scenario,
n_{R}\nu
SM, as discussed in chapter
2
.

The LHC has the capability to generate heavy neutrinos in the mass range of GeV/TeV by means of decays involving heavy mesons,
\tau
leptons,
W
bosons, Higgs bosons, and even top quarks. If there are novel interactions or gauge symmetries present in nature, or if the masses of heavy neutrinos exceed the electro-weak scale, then additional production mechanisms for heavy neutrinos become feasible. In the minimal model, the production of high-mass heavy neutrinos involves processes such as the Drell-Yan process
[
225
,
226
]
, gluon fusion
[
227
,
228
,
229
]
,
W\gamma
fusion
[
230
,
231
,
232
,
233
]
and same-sign WW fusion
[
234
,
235
]
. When neutrinos are Majorana particles, they can decay into both lepton number conserving and lepton number violating channels. However, if they are Dirac particles, only lepton number conserving processes are allowed. However, both Majorana and Dirac neutrinos can have lepton flavour violating processes. Furthermore, depending on the mass and mixing angle of the heavy neutrino, its decays can either be prompt (short lifetime) or displaced (long lifetime) from the production vertex. This section will only cover prompt decays, for displaced vertex decay searches see section
4.1.7
.

It is important to note that in the experimental searches for heavy neutrinos performed at the LHC, it is common to assume the presence of a single heavy neutrino mixing with a single neutrino flavour. Naturally, the exclusion limits obtained from these searches may not directly apply to the mixing angles in realistic neutrino models that can explain neutrino oscillations. It is evident that many of these searches require reinterpretation
[
236
]
.

4.1.2.1

M\sim
GeV/TeV

A CMS search from 2018
[
93
]
excluded at 95% confidence level heavy neutrinos with masses from
1
GeV up to
1.2
TeV for mixing
|V_{lN}|^{2}
from

up to 1.8, with
l=e,\mu

35.9\textrm{fb}^{-1}
. The results are displayed in Figure

.

Bounds for heavy neutrinos with masses below the GeV scale have better future prospects and are more stringent in other experiments that do not work in the energy frontier

4.1.3

LFV, LFNU and Electroweak Precision Observables

The presence of heavy almost-sterile neutrinos produces effects on electroweak precision observables, specifically lepton number and flavour violating decays
[
244
,
245
,
246
]
. Constraints on these typically do not rely on the mass of the heavy neutrinos, but it is important to consider the underlying assumptions made when deriving these constraints
[
57
]
. In Section
2.2.1
, it is explained that if all heavy neutrinos have masses above the electroweak scale (approximately
M\gtrsim m_{W}
, where
m_{W}
represents the mass of the W boson and
M
the mass scale of all heavy neutrinos), one can solely constrain the hermitian part of the leptonic mixing matrix, as defined in eq. (
2.79
).

According to the polar decomposition of a square invertible matrix, it is possible to express such a matrix as the product of a unitary matrix with a hermitian matrix. This hermitian matrix can be positioned either on the left or on the right of the unitary matrix
[
55
]
. In terms of notation, the hermitian part on the left is commonly written in terms of a matrix
\eta
, cf. eq. (
2.29
). The most stringent constraints on the
\eta
matrix are provided in equation (
2.1.1
), originating from references
[
52
,
56
,
192
]
. In particular, this discussion pertains to scenarios where the mass of the heavy neutrinos is around or above the GeV/TeV scale (
M\gtrsim
GeV/TeV), while more stringent bounds for lighter heavy neutrinos can be found in sections
4.1.1
,
4.1.4
and
4.1.5
.

In the
n_{R}\nu
SM, when all
M\gtrsim
GeV/TeV, the charged and neutral electroweak current are modified
[
56
]

j_{\mu}^{\pm}=l_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}K_{\alpha j}\nu_{j}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ j_{\mu}^{0}=\overline{\nu}_{i}(K^{\dagger}K)_{ij}\gamma_{\mu}\nu_{j}\penalty\ ,

(4.8)

where
l
are charged leptons,
\gamma
are the Gamma matrices,
\nu
are light active neutrinos and
K
is the
3\times 3
non-unitary leptonic mixing matrix, defined in eqs. (
2.8
) and (
2.14
). Using eq. (
2.14
) and comparing eq. (
4.8
) with eqs. (
2.7
), (
2.8
) and eqs. (
2.9
), (
2.11
) one can see indeed that eq. (
4.8
) is an approximation of the exact result, on which it is assumed that only the active light neutrinos enter in the low-energy electroweak processes one is studying. This is consistent with a effective description approach, where the heavy neutrinos are integrated out, compatible with what is known in the literature as the Minimum Unitarity Violation (MUV) scheme
[
56
]
. The
n_{R}\nu
SM along with all
M\gtrsim
GeV/TeV is equivalent to such approach and the decay rates and cross sections are proportional to
(KK^{\dagger})_{\alpha\beta}
and
\sum_{\beta}(KK^{\dagger})_{\alpha\beta}
for processes involving charged currents and
\sum_{ij}|(K^{\dagger}K)_{ij}|^{2}
for processes involving neutral currents.

The
\eta
matrix is commonly used in the literature because, using eq. (
2.79
), there is a straight forward connection between
KK^{\dagger}
, what appears in the charged currents, and
\eta

\displaystyle KK^{\dagger}\,=\,\mathds{1}-2\eta+\eta^{2}\approx\mathds{1}-2\eta\penalty\ ,

(4.9)

where the unitarity relation eq. (
2.15
) and eq. (
2.27
) were used.

Similarly, there is a connection between
K^{\dagger}K
, what appears in neutral currents, and
\eta

\displaystyle K^{\dagger}K\,=U_{K}^{\dagger}(\mathds{1}-2\eta+\eta^{2})U_{K}\approx\mathds{1}-2U_{K}^{\dagger}\eta U_{K}\penalty\ .

(4.10)

Neglecting the off-diagonal entries of
\eta
with respect to the diagonal entries, motivated by the Schwarz inequality for square hermitian matrices

|\eta_{\alpha\beta}|\leq\sqrt{\eta_{\alpha\alpha}\eta_{\beta\beta}}\penalty\ ,

(4.11)

and keeping only terms linear in
\eta_{ij}
, one can easily obtain simple expression for the factors that appear in charged currents

\sum_{\beta}(KK^{\dagger})_{\alpha\beta}\approx 1-2\eta_{\alpha\alpha}\approx(KK^{\dagger})_{\alpha\alpha}\penalty\ ,

(4.12)

(KK^{\dagger})_{\alpha\beta}=-2\eta_{\alpha\beta}\equiv\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \alpha\neq\beta\penalty\ ,

(4.13)

and neutral currents

\sum_{ij}|(K^{\dagger}K)_{ij}|^{2}\approx 3-4\eta_{11}-4\eta_{22}-4\eta_{33}\penalty\ ,

(4.14)

where the unitarity of
U_{K}
was used to obtain the last equation.

When
s
light heavy neutrinos are kinematically accessible, the usual bounds cannot be directly applied because the observables are no longer only proportional to
(KK^{\dagger})_{\alpha\beta}
,
\sum_{\beta=1}^{3}(KK^{\dagger})_{\alpha\beta}
and
\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}|(K^{\dagger}K)_{ij}|^{2}
but to

(KK^{\dagger})_{\alpha\beta}+(KX^{\dagger})_{\alpha s}(XK^{\dagger})_{s\beta}\penalty\ ,

\sum_{\beta=1}^{3}\Big((KK^{\dagger})_{\alpha\beta}+(KX^{\dagger})_{\alpha s}(XK^{\dagger})_{s\beta}\Big)

and

\sum_{i,j=1}^{3+s}\Bigg|\begin{pmatrix}K^{\dagger}K&K^{\dagger}KX^{\dagger}\\
XK^{\dagger}K&XK^{\dagger}KX^{\dagger}\\
\end{pmatrix}_{ij}\Bigg|^{2}\penalty\ ,

respectively
[
57
,
31
]
. An updated version of the study described in refs.
[
52
,
56
,
192
]
that takes into account such cases would be pertinent but maybe unfeasible in the present due to the expected smallness of the deviations from unitarity of the leptonic mixing matrix, which are proportional to the couplings among light active neutrinos and heavy almost-sterile neutrinos, cf. chapter
2
and ref.
[
31
]
.

A discussion on the most relevant observables that are sensitive to these effects follows.

4.1.3.1

Muon Decay:
G_{F}

The muon lifetime is one of the best measured quantities in particle physics
[
247
]
. The best measurement of
G_{F}
, which is one of most important parameters in the SM, is obtained from it. The decay rate of the muon in the
3n_{R}\nu
SM with all
M\gtrsim
GeV/TeV is approximately given by
[
52
,
56
,
192
]

\Gamma_{\mu}=\frac{m_{\mu}^{5}G_{F}^{2}}{192\pi^{3}}\sum_{i}(KK^{\dagger})_{2i}\sum_{j}(KK^{\dagger})_{1j}\approx\frac{m_{\mu}^{5}G_{F}^{2}}{192\pi^{3}}(1-2\eta_{11}-2\eta_{22})\equiv\frac{m_{\mu}^{5}G_{\mu}^{2}}{192\pi^{3}}\penalty\ ,

(4.15)

where eq. (
4.12
) was used and only terms linear in
\eta
were kept. In eq. (
4.15
) it was also implicitly defined

G_{\mu}=G_{F}\sqrt{(1-2\eta_{11}-2\eta_{22})}\approx G_{F}(1-\eta_{11}-\eta_{22})\penalty\ ,

(4.16)

which is
G_{F}
as determined through muon decay. Using the smallness of
\eta_{ii}
one can also obtain

G_{F}=G_{\mu}(1+\eta_{11}+\eta_{22})\penalty\ .

(4.17)

The present value of
G_{\mu}
is
[
3
]

G_{\mu}=1.1663788(6)\times 10^{-5}\penalty\ \textrm{GeV}^{-2}\penalty\ .

(4.18)

Hence, in this model, it acquires a non-unitary correction that spreads to other observables
[
52
,
56
,
192
]
. Thus, it is crucial to compare the measurement of
G_{F}
via the muon decay with other independent determinations of it
[
247
]
, like via super-allowed
\beta
decays, where similarly to eq. (
4.16
), one can define
G_{F}
determined through it as

G_{\beta}=G_{F}\sqrt{\sum_{j}(KK^{\dagger})_{1j}}\approx\sqrt{(1-2\eta_{11})}\approx G_{F}(1-\eta_{11})\penalty\ ,

(4.19)

where eq. (
4.12
) was used. It is important to keep in mind that one may be propagating non-unitary corrections to other measurements when using
G_{F}
determined through these weak processes.

4.1.3.2

Invisible
Z
width:
N_{\nu}

An important test to the number of active neutrino species with masses below the Z boson mass,
N_{\nu}
, was the measurement of the Z invisible width at LEP
[
248
]
, obtaining
N_{\nu}=2.984\pm 0.008
.
In the
3n_{R}\nu
SM with all
M\gtrsim
GeV/TeV the Z invisible width is approximately given by
[
52
,
56
,
192
]

\Gamma_{\textrm{inv}}=\frac{G_{F}M_{Z}^{3}}{12\sqrt{2}\pi}\sum_{ij}|(K^{\dagger}K)_{ij}|^{2}\approx\frac{G_{\mu}M_{Z}^{3}}{12\sqrt{2}\pi}(3-(4\eta_{33}+\eta_{11}+\eta_{22}))\equiv\frac{G_{\mu}M_{Z}^{3}}{12\sqrt{2}\pi}N_{\nu}\penalty\ ,

(4.20)

where eqs. (
4.14
), (
4.16
) and (
4.17
) were used. In eq. (
4.20
) it was also implicitly defined

N_{\nu}=3-(4\eta_{33}+\eta_{11}+\eta_{22})\penalty\ ,

(4.21)

The present value of
N_{\nu}
, averaged from several measurements
[
3
]
is

N_{\nu}=2.92\pm 0.05\penalty\ .

(4.22)

It is remarkable that the
n_{R}\nu
SM with all
M\gtrsim
GeV/TeV predicts this deviation from
3
, caused by the deviations from unitarity expressed in
\eta_{ii}
, cf. eq. (
4.21
).

4.1.3.3

W
Decay to leptons:
W\rightarrow l_{\alpha}\nu_{\alpha}

The decay width of the W boson into a charged lepton-neutrino pair in the
3n_{R}\nu
SM with all
M\gtrsim
GeV/TeV is given by
[
52
,
56
,
192
]

\Gamma_{W,\alpha}=\sum_{i}\Gamma(W\rightarrow l_{\alpha}\nu_{i})=\frac{G_{F}m_{W}^{3}}{6\sqrt{2}\pi}(KK^{\dagger})_{\alpha\alpha}F_{W}(m_{l_{\alpha}})\approx\frac{G_{\mu}m_{W}^{3}}{6\sqrt{2}\pi}F_{W}(m_{l_{\alpha}})(1+\eta_{22}+\eta_{11}-2\eta_{\alpha\alpha})\penalty\ ,

(4.23)

where eqs. (
4.12
) and (
4.17
) were used and with

F_{W}(m_{l_{\alpha}})=\Big(1-\frac{m^{2}_{l_{\alpha}}}{m^{2}_{W}}\Big)\Big(1+\frac{m^{2}_{l_{\alpha}}}{m^{2}_{W}}\Big)\penalty\ .

(4.24)

The flavour structure of this decay is common to many weak processes, hence its importance. Nevertheless, the
W
boson branching ratios to leptons is a well measured quantity
[
3
]
, making it a great test for non-unitarity.

4.1.3.4

LFNU: Lepton Flavour Universality Tests

From eq. (
4.23
) one can define the following lepton-universality observables

R_{\alpha\beta}^{W}=\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma_{W,\alpha}F_{W}(m_{l_{\beta}})}{\Gamma_{W,\beta}F_{W}(m_{l_{\alpha}}}}=\sqrt{\frac{(KK^{\dagger})_{\alpha\alpha}}{(KK^{\dagger})_{\beta\beta}}}\approx(1-\eta_{\alpha\alpha}+\eta_{\beta\beta})\penalty\ .

(4.25)

These observables allow to directly constrain the ratios of the diagonal elements of
\eta_{ii}
.
The flavour structure of
\Gamma_{W,\alpha}
, defined in eq. (
4.23
), is the same in the width for other weak processes such as
\pi/K\rightarrow l_{\alpha}\nu_{\alpha}
for instance, thus one can define
[
52
,
56
,
192
]

R_{\alpha\beta}=\sqrt{\frac{(KK^{\dagger})_{\alpha\alpha}}{(KK^{\dagger})_{\beta\beta}}}\approx(1-\eta_{\alpha\alpha}+\eta_{\beta\beta})\penalty\ ,

(4.26)

for ratios of decay widths of
W\rightarrow l_{\alpha}\nu_{\alpha}
processes, as in eq. (
4.23
), for ratios of decay widths of
l_{\alpha}\rightarrow l_{\beta}\nu_{\alpha}\overline{\nu}_{\beta}
processes or for ratios of decay widths of
K/\pi\rightarrow l_{\alpha}\overline{\nu}_{\alpha}
processes to
l_{\alpha}\rightarrow K/\pi\nu_{\alpha}
processes. Note that the following ratios of decay widths

R_{\mu e}=\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma(\tau\rightarrow\nu_{\tau}\mu\overline{\nu}_{\mu})}{\Gamma(\tau\rightarrow\nu_{\tau}e\overline{\nu}_{e})}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ R_{\tau\mu}=\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma(\tau\rightarrow\nu_{\tau}e\overline{\nu}_{e})}{\Gamma(\mu\rightarrow\nu_{\mu}e\overline{\nu}_{e})}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ R_{\mu e}=\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma(\pi\rightarrow\mu\overline{\nu}_{\mu})}{\Gamma(\pi\rightarrow e\overline{\nu}_{e})}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ R_{\tau\mu}=\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma(\tau\rightarrow K\nu_{\tau})}{\Gamma(K\rightarrow\mu\overline{\nu}_{\mu})}}\penalty\ ,

(4.27)

have the same flavour dependence, expressed in eq. (
4.26
). It is important to emphasize that in the SM
R_{\alpha\beta}
is given by a function of the masses of the involved charged leptons, incorporating phase space and chirality flip factors, along with various loop corrections. Since a flavour dependence is induced in the presence of non-unitary mixing, weak interaction lepton flavour universality constraints become powerful probes of light-heavy neutrino mixing.

4.1.3.5

Unitarity of the CKM Matrix

Both within the Standard Model (SM) and in the
n_{R}\nu
SM, the CKM matrix is unitary. Denoting the theoretical values of the CKM matrix elements with a superscript "th," the unitary condition for the first row can be expressed as follows

|V_{ud}^{\textrm{th}}|^{2}+|V_{us}^{\textrm{th}}|^{2}+|V_{ub}^{\textrm{th}}|^{2}=1\penalty\ .

(4.28)

As discussed in section
3.2
, presently a tension exists regarding the unitarity of the first row of the CKM matrix. This is known in the literature as the CKM unitarity problem or Cabibbo angle anomaly
[
24
,
25
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
]
.

The current experimental values for the entries of the first row of the CKM matrix are given in eq. (
3.69
). From those values, one can obtain the experimental version of eq. (
4.28
)
[
22
]
,

|V_{ud}^{\textrm{exp}}|^{2}+|V_{us}^{\textrm{exp}}|^{2}+|V_{ub}^{\textrm{exp}}|^{2}\approx 0.9984\pm 0.0001\penalty\ .

(4.29)

It is remarkable that in the MUV scheme, equivalent to the
3n_{R}\nu
SM with all
M\gtrsim
GeV/TeV, the weak processes involving leptons from which the
V_{ij}^{\textrm{exp}}
are measured get modified such that
[
52
,
56
,
192
]

|V_{ij}^{\textrm{exp}}|^{2}=|V_{ij}^{\textrm{th}}|^{2}(1+f^{\textrm{process}}(\eta_{\alpha\alpha}))\penalty\ ,

(4.30)

where
f^{\textrm{process}}
refers to a function of
\eta_{\alpha}
with
\alpha
the leptonic flavour involved in the process in which the CKM matrix element is measured.
For instance, in the determination of
|V_{ud}|
via super-allowed
\beta
decays, one has

|V_{ud}^{\textrm{exp},\beta}|^{2}\approx|V_{ud}^{\textrm{th}}|^{2}(1-2\eta_{11})(1+2\eta_{11}+2\eta_{22})\approx|V_{ud}^{\textrm{exp},\beta}|(1+2\eta_{22})\penalty\ ,

(4.31)

where eq. (
4.17
) and the smallness of
\eta_{ii}
was used, since the process is proportional to
G_{F}^{2}
, and the factor
(1-2\eta_{11})
comes from the electrons in the final state. A similar argument can be made regarding
V_{us}
and its measurement via kaon or tau decays
[
52
,
56
,
192
]

|V_{us}^{\textrm{exp},K\rightarrow\pi e\overline{\nu}_{e}}|^{2}\approx|V_{us}^{\textrm{th}}|^{2}(1+2\eta_{22})\penalty\ ,

(4.32)

|V_{us}^{\textrm{exp},K\rightarrow\pi\mu\overline{\nu}_{\mu}}|^{2}\approx|V_{us}^{\textrm{th}}|^{2}(1+2\eta_{11})\penalty\ ,

(4.33)

and

|V_{us}^{\textrm{exp},\tau\rightarrow K\nu_{\tau}}|^{2}\approx|V_{us}^{\textrm{th}}|^{2}(1+2\eta_{11}+2\eta_{22}-2\eta_{33})\penalty\ .

(4.34)

As explained in section
3.2
, the present accuracy and smallness of
V_{ub}
dictates that it can be safely neglected in eq. (
4.28
) and in this discussion, hence, when doing these types of studies, one can consider
|V_{ub}^{\textrm{exp}}|^{2}\equiv|V_{ub}^{\textrm{th}}|^{2}
.

The corollary of this section is that the measurement of
V_{\textrm{CKM}}
elements via weak processes involving leptons may propagate leptonic non-unitarity effects to the quark sector, which may alleviate the tension regarding the unitarity of the first row of the CKM matrix
[
249
,
250
]
.

4.1.3.6

LFV: Rare Charged Lepton Decays,
\mu\rightarrow e\gamma

Although they are absent in the SM due to the masslessness of neutrinos, charged lepton decays of the type
l_{\sigma}\rightarrow l_{\beta}\gamma
occur at one loop in the MUV scheme. The decay width for this LFV process is given by

\Gamma_{l_{\sigma}\rightarrow l_{\beta}\gamma}=\frac{\sigma G_{\mu}^{2}m_{\sigma}^{5}}{2048\pi}\Big|\sum_{j}K_{\sigma j}K_{j\beta}^{\dagger}F(x_{j})\Big|^{2}\penalty\ ,

(4.35)

where
\alpha
is the fine structure constant,
m_{\sigma}
is the mass of the charged lepton with flavour
\sigma
and the terms of order
\mathcal{O}(m_{l_{\sigma}}/m_{l_{\beta}})^{2})
were neglected.
F(x_{j})
is a loop function where
x_{j}=m_{j}/m_{W}\approx 0
, where
m_{i}
are the masses of the light neutrinos. The smallness but non-zero nature of
F(x_{j})
allows the excellent approximation

\sum_{j}K_{\sigma j}K_{j\beta}^{\dagger}F(x_{j})\approx-2F(0)\eta_{\sigma\beta}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ F(0)=\frac{10}{3}\penalty\ .

(4.36)

Using this approximation one can write simple formulas for the branching ratios of the LFV processes
l_{\sigma}\rightarrow l_{\beta}\gamma

[
52
,
56
,
192
]

\textrm{BR}(l_{\sigma}\rightarrow l_{\beta}\gamma)=G_{\sigma\beta}\frac{100\alpha}{96\pi}4|\eta_{\sigma\beta}|^{2}\penalty\ ,

(4.37)

where
G_{\sigma\beta}
is a numerical factor that contains phase space factors

G_{\mu e}=1\penalty\ ,\penalty\ G_{\tau e}=\frac{1}{5.6}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ G_{\tau\mu}=\frac{1}{5.9}\penalty\ .

(4.38)

As pointed out in section
2.2.1
, the experimental bound obtained from the search for the LFV decay
\mu\rightarrow e\gamma
is one of the most stringent constraints on the parameter space of
n_{R}\nu
SM models with sizeable deviations from unitarity. Hence, the search by the MEG collaboration
[
95
]
and also for the other rare tau decays
\tau\rightarrow e\gamma
,
\tau\rightarrow\mu\gamma

[
251
]
provide a great probe for the non-unitarity of the leptonic mixing matrix and one of the best ways to constrain the off-diagonal terms of
\eta
.

4.1.4

Signals in Neutrino Oscillation Experiments

Neutrino oscillation experiments, such as SNO
[
7
]
and Super-Kamiokande
[
6
]
, played a crucial role in the discovery that neutrinos have mass. These experiments are known as long-baseline (LBL) experiments and are characterized by having a large source-detector distance, often denominated as
L
, allowing for oscillations due to extremely small mass differences to develop, leading to a sufficient phase difference to cause flavour transformations
[
252
]
. T2K
[
253
]
and No
\nu
A
[
254
]
are prominent examples.

In contrast, short-baseline (SBL) experiments, as implied by their name, involve significantly shorter source-detector distances, typically on the order of a few meters
[
255
]
, as is the case of MiniBooNE
[
256
]
.

Considering a unitary leptonic mixing matrix,
U\equiv U_{\textrm{PMNS}}
, defined in eq. (
2.1
), the oscillation probability between a (anti-)neutrino of flavour
\alpha
to a (anti-)neutrino of flavour
\beta
, with
\alpha,\beta=e,\mu,\tau
, can be shown to take the form

\displaystyle P_{\hbox{\hskip 12.41393pt\hskip-2.00464pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.00464pt\hskip-12.41393pt\raisebox{4.06389pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-12.41393pt\hskip 12.41393pt}_{\alpha}\rightarrow\hbox{\hskip 12.41393pt\hskip-2.00464pt\hbox{$\nu$}\hskip-2.00464pt\hskip-12.41393pt\raisebox{4.06389pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{.3}{(}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\bf--}\scalebox{.3}{)}}}\hskip-12.41393pt\hskip 12.41393pt}_{\beta}}(L,E)\,=\,\delta_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle-4\sum_{i>j}^{3+n}\,\re\left(U_{\alpha i}^{*}\,U_{\beta i}\,U_{\alpha j}\,U_{\beta j}^{*}\right)\sin^{2}\Delta_{ij}

(4.39)

\displaystyle\pm 2\sum_{i>j}^{3+n}\,\im\left(U_{\alpha i}^{*}\,U_{\beta i}\,U_{\alpha j}\,U_{\beta j}^{*}\right)\sin 2\Delta_{ij}\,,

where the plus or minus sign in the second line refers to neutrinos or anti-neutrinos, respectively.
Here, again
L
denotes the source-detector distance,
E
is the (anti-)neutrino energy,
and one has defined

\Delta_{ij}\,\equiv\,\frac{\Delta m^{2}_{ik}\,L}{4E}\,\simeq\,1.27\,\frac{\Delta m_{ij}^{2}[\text{eV}^{2}]\,L[\text{km}]}{E[\text{GeV}]}\,,

(4.40)

with mass-squared differences
\Delta m_{ij}^{2}\equiv m_{i}^{2}-m_{j}^{2}
. In order for experiments to be sensitive to neutrino oscillations, it is necessary for the neutrino energy and the distance to the detector to satisfy the condition
\Delta_{ij}\,\equiv\,\frac{\Delta m^{2}_{ik}\,L}{4E}\gtrsim 1
. However, it is often impractical to physically move either the detector or the source to cover a wide range of
L/E
values. Therefore, having a broad energy spectrum proves advantageous as it allows for the detection of oscillations through the distortion of the expected neutrino energy distribution, as depicted in figure
4.7
, which is a reconstructed
\nu_{\mu}
energy spectrum from the MINOS experiment
[
257
]
.

Figure 4.7
:
Distribution of muon neutrino events plotted as a function of reconstructed energy in the MINOS far detector at a distance of
735
km. The
solid grey line shows the expected spectrum under no oscillation hypothesis. The
depletion of events at
\gtrsim 1.5
GeV and the shape of the depleted spectrum is
consistent with dominant
\nu_{\mu}\rightarrow\nu_{\tau}
oscillations with maximal mixing. Image taken from ref.
[
252
]
.

The sensitivity ranges for different types of neutrino experiments are as follows:

•

For solar neutrino experiments (
L\approx 10^{11}
m,
E\approx 15
MeV), the sensitivity range is
\Delta m_{ij}\approx 10^{-10}\penalty\ \textrm{eV}^{2}
.

•

For atmospheric neutrino experiments (
20
km
<L<10^{3}
km,
E\approx 1
GeV), the sensitivity range is
\Delta m_{ij}\approx 10^{-4}\penalty\ \textrm{eV}^{2}
.

•

For reactor neutrino experiments (
L\approx[10,10^{4}]
m,
E\approx 3
MeV), the sensitivity range is
\Delta m_{ij}\approx 10^{-5}\penalty\ \textrm{eV}^{2}
.

•

For accelerator neutrino experiments (
L\leq 500
km,
E\approx 1
GeV), the sensitivity range is
\Delta m_{ij}\approx 10^{-3}\penalty\ \textrm{eV}^{2}
.

•

For a typical SBL experiment (
L\sim\mathcal{O}(100)
m,
E\approx\mathcal{O}(100)
MeV), the sensitivity range is
\Delta m_{ij}\approx\mathcal{O}(1)\penalty\ \textrm{eV}^{2}
.

One cannot end this summary without mentioning IceCube
[
258
]
, an experiment that is sensitive to typical LBL
\Delta m_{ij}

[
259
]
but also to the usual SBL
\Delta m_{ij}

[
260
]
, as it can detect both cosmic and atmospheric neutrinos, hence being able to acquire data for various values of
L/E
.

Comprehensive summaries of current results based on global fits from LBL experiments, considering a unitary leptonic mixing matrix, can be found in references
[
261
,
59
]
. The results from ref.
[
59
]
can be found in Table
2.2
. These results provide information about the leptonic mixing angles and the Dirac phase
\delta
. However, the models studied in this thesis do not have unitary leptonic mixing matrices, as discussed in chapter
2
. Consequently, the equations governing neutrino oscillation probabilities, such as eq. (
4.39
), are modified due to non-unitarity effects, cf. section
2.2
and eq. (
2.45
). This modified equation can be simplified for specific cases, such as SBL and LBL experimental setups with the consideration of matter effects in LBL experiments being mandatory in order to obtain realistic results.

Interestingly, experiments with sensitivities of
\Delta m_{ij}\sim\mathcal{O}(1)\penalty\ \textrm{eV}^{2}
are particularly susceptible to non-unitarity effects
[
31
]
, as discussed in section
2.2
. Notably, SBL experiments, which fall within this sensitivity range
[
262
]
, observed an anomaly now known as SBL anomaly, initially referred to as the LSND anomaly
[
74
,
75
]
, based on the experiment that detected it. This anomaly involves an excess of
\overline{\nu}_{e}
at the detector from a
\overline{\nu}_{\mu}
source. The
L/E
settings of the detector points towards a large
\Delta m^{2}
if interpreted as a two-neutrino oscillation
\overline{\nu}_{\mu}\xrightarrow{}\overline{\nu}_{e}
.
Reactor and radioactive source experiments also reported excess or deficit of
\nu_{e}
or
\overline{\nu}_{e}
in the detector
[
76
,
77
,
78
,
263
]
. Later, KARMEN reported results that disagreed with the results from LSND
[
62
]
, but the MiniBOONE experiment

[
256
]
confirmed the results obtained by LSND
[
79
,
80
]
. MiniBOONE, despite having similar
L/E
as LSND, differs in terms of energy, beam, detector systematics, event signatures and backgrounds, which makes it an excellent tester of the LSND results.

A global fit combining all these results and other constraints
[
264
,
66
]
suggests the existence of a heavy sterile neutrino with the data exhibiting a preference for the
(3+1)
scenario, where there are three light neutrinos and one neutrino with a mass in the eV range and a sizeable coupling with at least one light neutrino
[
72
,
65
,
265
]
. Hence, these anomalies may be explained if the spectrum of heavy neutrinos includes at least one neutrino with an eV-scale mass
[
78
,
64
]
, and if there are significant deviations from the unitarity of the
3\times 3
mixing matrix (DU)

[
71
,
31
]
.

However, it is crucial to note that a closer examination of the dataset reveals tension between the appearance and disappearance datasets, as well as the neutrino and anti-neutrino datasets
[
81
]
. Furthermore, recently, the IceCube experiment also reported results that conflict with the ones from LSND and MiniBOONE
[
260
]
.
In conclusion, all of these anomalies and hypotheses are still subject to ongoing debate and discussion.

4.1.4.1

Effects in LBL Experiments

Obtaining direct evidence of heavy neutrinos in LBL oscillation experiments is extremely challenging because these experiments are sensitive to very small mass differences,
\Delta m_{ij}
. One possibility for detection would arise if there exists a pair of quasi-degenerate heavy neutrinos, hence with a small enough mass splitting, with significant mixing and adequate mass in order for it to be produced and/or detectable in the experiment. For the spectra considered in this thesis, which contain a quasi-degenerate pair of heavy neutrinos with TeV masses, cf. case
II
of
2.2
, an experiment like IceCube
[
266
,
258
]
could potentially detect them, although the likelihood of this occurring is extremely low.

LBL experiments have a higher probability of detecting non-unitarity effects associated with large mass splittings. This is because terms proportional to
\sin^{2}(\Delta m\penalty\ L/4E)
can be effectively averaged out to approximately 0.5
[
86
]
, thus the oscillation probabilities in these cases only depend on the usual mixing among light neutrinos and the mixing between light and heavy neutrinos. The modifications of neutrino oscillation probabilities in vacuum in LBL experiments for the cases studied in ref.
[
31
]
is discussed in section
2.2.2
. Using the definitions of eq. (
2.52
) and eq. (
2.61
), the transition probability of muon to electron (anti-)neutrinos for the
Ia
case can be approximated by eq. (
2.60
), for the
Ib
case by eq. (
2.64
) and for the
II
case by eq. (
2.74
).

Hence, heavy almost-sterile neutrinos may leave traces in LBL experiments. This should come as no surprise since, as stated earlier in this section, in the presence of a non-unitary mixing matrix, and possibly a sizeable mixing with almost-sterile neutrinos, the neutrino oscillation probabilities are modified. This can have noticeable effects in the conversion and survival probabilities of a neutrino species in a LBL experiment
[
192
]
, mostly due to the effect of the hermitian phases, the phases of the deviations from unitarity, first discussed in this thesis in section
2.3.1
.
A summary of the effect of these phases in CP Violation measurements can also be found in section
4.1.6
. However, the limited sensitivity of active LBL experiments to these effects, see ref.
[
86
]
and the relatively weak limits obtained, means that perhaps only experiments like DUNE
[
267
,
107
]
have the potential to truly test them.

Nevertheless, is interesting to point out that a global-fit from a combined analysis of 2019 and 2020 data from T2K and NO
\nu
A prefers non-unitarity at 2
\sigma
C.L.
[
17
]
. The tension between NO
\nu
A and T2K 2019 data is also reduced when
both the experiments are analysed with the non-unitary hypothesis. If in the future the two experiments consistently show improved agreement with the non-unitary hypothesis, it could serve as a compelling indication of the existence of new physics in the neutrino sector.

4.1.4.2

Effects in SBL Experiments

SBL experiments and LBL experiments have distinct discovery prospects. SBL experiments are capable of detecting oscillations only when the magnitude of
\Delta m_{ij}
falls within their typical sensitivity range, which is approximately
\Delta m_{ij}\approx\mathcal{O}(1)\textrm{eV}^{2}
. Consequently, SBL experiments can test the existence of almost-sterile neutrinos with a mass of around
1
eV, as in case
Ia
of ref.
[
31
]
, or the presence of a pair of nearly-sterile neutrinos with
\Delta m_{ij}\approx\mathcal{O}(1)\textrm{eV}^{2}
, as outlined in case
Ib
of ref.
[
31
]
.
The observability of these oscillations is enhanced when there is a substantial mixing between the heavy and light neutrinos, as demonstrated by the figures and benchmarks presented in section
2.2.2
and reference
[
31
]
. In fact, the resolution of the aforementioned SBL anomalies, within the 3+1 scheme, which closely resembles the
Ia
case, requires
\Delta m^{2}=0.209
eV
2
and
\sin^{2}2\vartheta^{(4)}_{\mu e}=0.0316
and
|V_{e4}|^{2}=0.016
, a 4.3
\sigma
preference comparing to the no-oscillation scenario, per the most recent work of the MiniBooNE and MicroBooNE collaborations
[
268
]
, cf. figure
4.8
.

Figure 4.8
:
The results of the MiniBooNE-only and combined
fits with MicroBooNE’s CCQE sample
[
269
]
(top) and MicroBooNE’s Inclusive sample
[
270
]
(bottom). The likelihood is
obtained by profiling over all parameters
\Delta m^{2}
and
\sin^{2}(2\theta_{\mu e})\equiv\sin^{2}2\vartheta^{(4)}_{\mu e}
. The two best-fit points are shown as appropriately
colored stars, and the contours are obtained by comparing the
profile-likelihood-ratio test-statistic to the asymptotic distribution provided by Wilks’ theorem, and assuming a difference of two degrees of freedom. Image taken from ref.
[
268
]
.

MicroBooNE
[
108
]
is another SBL experiment built in the same apparatus as MiniBooNE, located in the Booster Neutrino Beam Beamline at Fermilab. MicroBooNE’s two main physics goals are to investigate the MiniBooNE low-energy excess and neutrino-argon cross sections.

The modifications of neutrino oscillation probabilities in vacuum for the cases examined in ref.
[
31
]
are discussed in section
2.2.2
. By utilizing the definitions in equation (
2.52
) and equation (
2.61
), the transition probability from muon to electron (anti-)neutrinos can be approximated as equation (
2.63
) for the
Ia
case, equation (
2.66
) for the
Ib
case, and equation (
2.76
) for the
II
case.
Hence, SBL experiments provide the most stringent constraints on light heavy neutrinos with eV-scale masses
[
57
]
. In these cases, the observables directly constrain the entries of the mixing matrix
V_{\alpha k}=(KX^{\dagger})_{\alpha k}
, where
\alpha=1,2,3
and
k=1,..,n_{R}
, defined in eq. (
2.8
) and eq. (
2.14
). To incorporate these constraints from the experiments, the relevant exclusion curves in the
\sin^{2}2\vartheta{\alpha\beta}
–
\Delta m^{2}
planes are considered and translated into constraints on the elements of the mixing matrix
V_{\alpha k}
.

For values
\Delta m^{2}\in[10^{-2},1]\textrm{eV}^{2}
the combined result of MINOS
[
83
]
, MINOS+
[
271
]
, Daya Bay
[
272
]
and Bugey-3
[
82
]
gives the best result, shown in fig.
4.9
.

Figure 4.9
:
The combined limits at 99% C.L.(CLs) on
\sin^{2}(2\theta_{\mu e})\equiv\sin^{2}2\vartheta{\alpha\beta}
using the CLs method (red). The exclusion
contours are compared to the LSND and MiniBooNE allowed regions. Also shown are the allowed regions from global fits by Gariazzo et al.
[
105
]
and Dentler et al.
[

]
. Note that
\sin^{2}(2\theta_{\mu e})\equiv\sin^{2}2\vartheta^{(4)}_{\mu e}
. Image taken from ref.
[
273
]
.

Thus, for a heavy neutrino with a
1
eV mass and
\Delta m^{2}\sim 1\textrm{eV}^{2}
, one has the bound

\sin^{2}2\vartheta^{(4)}_{\mu e}=4|V_{e4}|^{2}|V_{\mu 4}|^{2}\lesssim 10^{-3}\penalty\ .

(4.41)

Note that the benchmarks presented in tables
6a
,
6b
and
6c
satisfy this bound.

For larger values of
\Delta m^{2}
, KARMEN
[
274
]
, MiniBooNE
[
80
]
and MicroBooNE
[
275
]
give the most stringent limit, depicted in fig.
4.10
.

preferred regions is shown in shades of grey
[

]
, the future sensitivity of

[

and OPERA (gold)
[

]
. Note that
\sin^{2}(2\theta_{\mu e})\equiv\sin^{2}2\vartheta^{(4)}_{\mu e}
. Image taken from ref.
[

]
.

Note that a large portion of the LSND and MiniBooNE allowed region
[

]
and the 3+1 scheme best-fit zone
[
